Extended Stone Duality via Monoidal Adjunctions
Fabian Lenke ORCID:0000-0001-5890-9485, Henning Urbat ORCID:0000-0002-3265-7168, Stefan Milius ORCID:0000-0002-2021-1644

Abstract
. Extensions of Stone-type dualities have a long history in algebraic logic and have also been instrumental in proving results in algebraic language theory. We show how to extend abstract categorical dualities via monoidal adjunctions, subsuming various incarnations of classical extended Stone and Priestley duality as special cases, and providing the foundation for two new concrete dualities: First, we investigate residuation algebras, which are lattices with additional residual operators modeling language derivatives algebraically. We show that the subcategory of derivation algebras is dually equivalent to the category of profinite ordered monoids, restricting to a duality between Boolean residuation algebras and profinite monoids. We further refine this duality to capture relational morphisms of profinite ordered monoids, which dualize to natural morphisms of residuation algebras. Second, we apply the categorical extended duality to the discrete setting of sets and complete atomic Boolean algebras to obtain a concrete description for the dual of the category of all small categories.



1.
Introduction

Marshall H. Stone’s representation theorem for Boolean algebras
[
Sto36
]
, the
foundation for the so called
Stone duality
between Boolean
algebras and Stone spaces, manifests a tight connection between logic
and topology. It has thus become an ubiquitous tool in various areas
of theoretical computer science, not only in logic, but also, for
example, in domain theory
[
Abr91
]
and automata theory
[
Pip97
,
GGP08
]
.

From algebraic logic arose the need for extending Stone duality to
capture Boolean algebras equipped with additional operators (modelling
quantifiers or modalities). Originating from Jónsson and Tarski’s
representation theorem for Boolean algebras with
operators
[
JT51
,
JT52
]
, a representation
in the spirit of Stone was proven by Halmos
[
Hal58
]
; the
general categorical picture of the duality of Kripke frames and modal
algebras is based on an adjunction between operators and continuous
relations developed by Sambin and Vaccaro
[
SV88
]
.

In the context of automata theory, the need for extensions of Stone
duality was only unveiled in this millennium:
Using ordinary Stone duality, Pippenger
[
Pip97
]
(see also Almeida
[
Alm94
]
) has already shown that the Boolean
algebra of regular languages on an alphabet
\Sigma
corresponds
to the Stone space
\widehat{\Sigma^{*}}
of
profinite words. This result and the theory surrounding it was placed in the bigger picture by categorical frameworks that have identified Stone-type dualities to be one of the cornerstones of
algebraic language theory
[
UACM17
,
Sal17
,
Blu21
]
.
On the other hand, Gehrke et al.
[
GGP08
]

discovered that, under Goldblatt’s form of extended Priestley
duality
[
Gol89
]
, the
residuals
of language concatenation dualize to
multiplication
of profinite words, but so far this result could not yet be placed in the categorical big picture. One
reason might be that, despite some progress in recent
years
[
BKR07
,
HN15
]
, extended (Stone)
dualities for (co-)algebras are themselves not fully understood as
instances of a crisp categorical idea.

Contributions

In the present paper, we introduce in
Section 3
a simple categorical framework for extending any categorical duality

\mathbf{C}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
via
monoidal adjunctions
: for
a given adjunction on
\mathbf{C}
with a strong monoidal right adjoint

U
, we establish a dual equivalence between

U
-operators on
\mathbf{C}
and operators in a Kleisli
category on
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
.
This framework is compositional both in its parameter – the strong monoidal right adjoint – and the object level of
U
-operators, which yields a simple categorical version of correspondence theory.

We demonstrate the power of this framework by working out several examples.
On one hand, we show how to recover existing dualities and applications thereof, but we also indicate how to extend existing dualities including completely new instances of dualities.

To this end, we show in
Section 4
how to use our framework to recover,
and mildly extend, extended Priestley duality for distributive lattices with
operators
[
Gol89
]
and relational morphisms. We also show how to use the
compositionality of the abstract extended duality to recover results from modal correspondence
theory for free.

Guided by our
categorical foundations for extended Stone duality, we subsequently investigate in
Section 5
the
correspondence between language derivatives and multiplication of profinite words
in the setting of
residuation algebras
.
The key observation is that, on
complete and completely distributive lattices, the residuals are equivalent to a

coalgebraic
operator on the lattice. This equivalence can then be composed with an extended duality based on the discrete duality between complete atomic Boolean algebras (CABAs) and sets to obtain a duality between certain complete residuation algebras and ordered monoids.
We then proceed to lift
this correspondence to locally finite structures, i.e. structures
built up from finite substructures. By identifying suitable non-full
subcategories – derivation algebras and (lattice) comonoids,
respectively – and an appropriate definition of morphism for
residuation algebras, we augment Gehrke’s characterization of
Stone-topological algebras in terms of residuation algebras to a
duality between the categories of derivation algebras and that of
profinite ordered monoids:

\mathbf{Der}\;\cong\;\mathbf{Comon}\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\mathbf{ProfOrdMon}.

(1.1)

The abstract theory of extended duality now suggests that the
dual equivalence between profinite ordered monoids on the one side and comonoids
as well as derivation algebras on the other side extends to a more general
duality capturing morphisms of
relational
type of profinite ordered monoids.
To this end, we identify a natural notion of relational
morphism for residuation algebras and comonoids, and we use our abstract extended duality theorem to obtain the dual equivalence

\mathbf{RelDer}\;\cong\;\mathbf{RelComon}\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\mathbf{RelProfOrdMon}

which extends (
1.1
) to relational morphisms.

Finally, we combine the ideas underlying these results
to derive a novel duality between the
category of all small categories and the category of
categorical residuation algebras
:

\mathbf{Cat}\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\mathbf{CatResCABA}.

(1.2)

To achieve this, we use the duality between monoids and residuation CABAs and combine it with two observations:
first, small categories are equivalent to certain
relational monoids
;
second, the discrete version of the duality (
1.1
) admits an extension to a duality between relational
monoids and
residuation CABAs
.
We prove that the composite of these equivalences restricts to (
1.2
).

Related Work.

This paper is a completely revised and extended version of our
conference paper
[
BMU24
]
presented at FoSSaCS 2024. Besides providing detailed proofs
of all results, we have included additional material:

6
simplifies dualization of composite operators,
and it is used in
Section 4.3
to show how to derive results from modal correspondence theory
in a categorical way, by encoding modal formulas as morphisms.

Section 5.3
has been extended to complete lattices, instead of just finite ones, to set the base duality for the material in
Section 6
.
We have added
45
to complete the picture
relating profinite ordered monoids and Priestley monoids similarly to the unordered case. Finally, the dual characterization of the category
\mathbf{Cat}
of small categories (
Section 6
) is a new application of our abstract methods.

Extended Stone Duality.

Duality for (complete) Boolean algebras with operators goes back to
Jónsson and Tarski
[
JT51
,
JT52
]
. This
duality was refined by the topological approach via Stone spaces taken
by Halmos
[
Hal58
]
, which allowed to characterize the
relations arising as the duals of operators, namely
Boolean
relations
. Halmos’ duality was extended to distributive lattices
with (
n
-ary) operators by Goldblatt
[
Gol89
]
and
Cignoli
[
CLP91
]
. Kupke et al.
[
KKV04
]

recognized that Boolean relations elegantly describe descriptive
frames as coalgebras for the (underlying functor of) the Vietoris monad on
the category of Stone spaces; notions of bisimulation for these coalgebras were
investigated by Bezhanishvili
et al.
[
BFV10
]
. Bonsangue
et al.
[
BKR07
]
introduced a framework for
dualities over distributive lattices equipped with a theory of
operators for a signature, which are dual to certain coalgebras.
Hofmann and Nora
[
HN15
]
have taken a categorical approach
to extend natural dualities to algebras for a signature equipped with
unary operators preserving only some of the operations
prescribed by the signature; they relate these to coalgebras for (the
underlying functor of) a suitable monad
T
. Recent work by Bezhanishvili
et al.
[
BHM23
]
clarifies the relation
between free constructions on distributive lattices and different
versions of the Vietoris monad to derive several dualities for
distributive lattices with different types of operators and their
corresponding Priestley relations.

Similarly to Hofmann and Nora’s work
[
HN15
]
we have tried to fit most of these
developments in a single, categorical framework. Our approach differs in two ways: first,
incorporating monoidal structures into our setting immediately allows us to dualize operators
with multiple in- and outputs. Second, besides a “nice” base duality, our framework depends
only on some monoidal right adjoint. In contrast, op. cit. requires,
in addition to the right adjoint (represented by a subsignature), a candidate monad
T

and gives a criterion whether an extended duality between operators for the subsignature and

T
-coalgebras exists.

Residuation Algebras.

The original work by Jónsson and Tarski
[
JT51
]
already used duality for residual operators (also called
conjugates
) on Boolean algebras. Residuated Boolean algebras, i.e. Boolean algebras with a residuated
binary operator, were explicitly studied by Jónsson and
Tsinakis
[
JT93
]
to highlight the role of the residuals in
relation algebra. Gehrke et al.
[
GGP08
]

exposed the connection between the residuals of the concatenation
of regular languages and the multiplication on profinite words and
investigated applications to automata theory, most notably a
duality-theoretic proof of Eilenberg’s variety
theorem
[
Eil76
]
. The duality theory behind the
correspondence of general residuation algebras and
Priestley-topological algebras developed by Gehrke
[
Geh16b
]

is based on Goldblatt’s
extension of Stone duality
[
Gol89
]
for distributive lattices.
This duality was further investigated via the theory of canonical
extensions
[
GJ94
,
Geh09
,
GP07
]
to show that certain crucial parts are not entirely “algebraic”:
While Gehrke
[
Geh16b
]
provides a condition under which the dual relation of the
residuals is functional,
Fussner and Palmigiano
[
FP19
]
showed that it cannot be equationally defined in the language of
residuation algebras.

Our duality for Priestley monoids is a non-trivial restriction of Gehrke’s
duality
[
Geh16b
]
, and, to our knowledge, the first duality result for relational
morphisms of profinite monoids, which are a ubiquitous tool in algebraic language
theory
[
Pin88
]
and semigroup theory
[
RS09
]
. We also note that, while our
results are closely related to Gehrke’s
[
Geh16b
]
, our methods are fundamentally
different: while most of the proofs in op. cit. are of topological nature, we only work
on the side of ordered structures, and outsource any topology to already existing dualities. In
our opinion, this not only simplifies the theory, but it also clarifies the relation between
Gehrke’s results and the duality by Rhodes and Steinberg
[
RS09
]
between profinite
monoids and counital Boolean bialgebras: on the algebraic side, derivation algebras correspond
to Boolean comonoids, which are precisely counital Boolean bialgebras.

2.
Preliminaries

Readers are assumed to be familiar with basic category theory, such as
functors, natural transformations, adjunctions and monoidal
categories, see Mac Lane
[
ML98
]
for an introduction.

We
briefly recall the foundations of Stone duality
[
Sto36
]
and
Priestley duality
[
Pri70
]
. By the latter we mean the dual
equivalence
\mathbf{DL}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{Priest}
between the category
\mathbf{DL}
of
bounded distributive lattices and lattice homomorphisms, and the
category
\mathbf{Priest}
of Priestley spaces (ordered compact topological
spaces in which for every
x\not\leq y
there exists a clopen upset
containing
x
but not
y
) and continuous monotone maps. The duality
maps a distributive lattice
D
to the pointwise-ordered space

\mathbf{DL}(D,2)
of homomorphisms into the two-element lattice
(equivalently, prime filters, ordered by inclusion), and topologized
via pointwise convergence. In the reverse direction, it maps a
Priestley space
X
to the distributive lattice
\mathbf{Priest}(X,2)
of
continuous maps into the two-element poset
2=\{0\leq 1\}
with
discrete topology (equivalently, clopen upsets), with the pointwise
lattice structure. Priestley duality restricts to Stone duality

\mathbf{BA}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{Stone}
between the full subcategories
\mathbf{BA}
of
Boolean algebras and
\mathbf{Stone}
of Stone spaces (discretely ordered
Priestley spaces).

This duality also has a “topologically discrete” version:
First recall that an element
k
in a lattice
D
is
compact
if for every subset
A\subseteq D
with
k\leq\bigvee A
there exists a finite subset
F\subseteq A
with
k\leq F
. A lattice is
algebraic
if every element is the join of the compact elements below it.
There exists a duality
\mathbf{Pos}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{ACDL}

between the category
\mathbf{Pos}
of posets with order-preserving maps and the category
\mathbf{ACDL}
of algebraic completely distributive lattices (ACDLs) with maps preserving all joins and meets. Under this duality a poset
P
is mapped to the set
\mathbf{Pos}(P,2)\cong\mathcal{D}P
, which corresponds to the set of downsets of
P
. In the reverse direction, an ACDL
D
is mapped to the pointwise-ordered poset
\mathbf{ACDL}(D,2)\cong\mathcal{J}P
, which corresponds to the poset of completely join-prime elements of
D
, i.e. those elements of
D
satisfying
p\leq\bigvee A\Rightarrow p\leq a
for some
a\in A
.
This duality restricts to the well-known duality
\mathbf{Set}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{CABA}
between the category
\mathbf{Set}
and the category
\mathbf{CABA}
of complete atomic boolean algebras.

Both the topological and the discrete duality restrict to Birkhoff
duality
[
Bir37
]

\mathbf{DL}_{\mathrm{f}}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{Pos}_{\mathrm{f}}
between finite
distributive lattices and finite posets. For a comprehensive
introduction to ordered structures and their dualities, see the first
two chapters of the classic textbook by Johnstone
[
Joh82
]
.

3.
Extending Dualities

Our first contribution is a general categorical framework for extending Stone-type dualities via monoidal adjunctions. It is motivated by the extension of Priestley duality to operators due to Goldblatt
[
Gol89
]
(which is recovered in
Section 4
) and serves as the basis for several concrete duality results derived subsequently.

We start this chapter by setting up some notation for the two ingredients involved: adjunctions and monoidal categories.

{nota}

(1)

Given functors
U\colon\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
and

F\colon\mathbf{D}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
, we write

F\colon\mathbf{D}\dashv\mathbf{C}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU

(or simply

F\dashv U
) if
F
is left adjoint to
U
. We denote the unit
and counit by

\eta\colon\Id\to UF\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varepsilon\colon FU\to\Id;

(3.1)

the transposing isomorphisms
are denoted by

(-)^{+}\colon\mathbf{D}(C,UD)\rightleftarrows\mathbf{C}(FC,D)\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu(-)^{-}

Hence, for every
f\colon C\to UD
and
g\colon FC\to D
we have

f^{+}=\varepsilon_{D}\cdot Ff,\qquad\text{and}\qquad g^{-}=Ug\cdot\eta_{C}.

(2)

For a category
\mathbf{C}
with dual
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
we
denote both contravariant functors witnessing the dual equivalence by

(\widehat{-})\colon\mathbf{C}\xrightarrow{~\simeq~}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\widehat{-})\colon\widehat{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{~\simeq~}\mathbf{C}.

Moreover,
if
F\colon\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
is a functor and
\widehat{\mathbf{D}}

is dual to
\mathbf{D}
, then we denote the dual of
F
by

\widehat{F}=(\widehat{-})\cdot F\cdot(\widehat{-})\colon\widehat{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{D}}.

(3)

The Kleisli category of a monad
(T,\eta,\mu)
on the
category
\mathbf{C}

is denoted by
\mathbf{C}_{T}
. It has the same objects as

\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C}_{T}(X,Y)=\mathbf{C}(X,TY)
; the
composition of
Kleisli morphisms

f\colon C\to TD
and

g\colon D\to TE
is defined by

g\cdot f=\big(C\xrightarrow{~f~}TD\xrightarrow{~Tg~}TTE\xrightarrow{~\mu_{E}~}TE\big).

A Kleisli morphism

f\colon C\rightarrow TD
is

pure
if
f=\eta_{D}\cdot f^{\prime}
for some
f^{\prime}\colon C\to D

in
\mathbf{C}
. We write
J_{T}\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}_{T}
for the usual identity-on-objects functor
mapping
f\colon A\to B
to
\eta_{B}\cdot f\colon A\to TB
.

{conv}

To lighten notation, we omit subscripts indicating components of
natural transformations when they are clear from the context,
e.g. we write
\eta\colon A\to TA
for
\eta_{A}
.

{defi}

(1)

A
monoidal category
is a category
\mathbf{C}
with a bifunctor
\otimes\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
called
tensor
, an object
I\in\mathbf{C}
called
unit
together with natural isomorphisms

\upsilon\colon I\otimes\Id_{\mathbf{C}}\cong\Id_{\mathbf{C}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\alpha\colon(\Id_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\Id_{\mathbf{C}})\otimes\Id_{\mathbf{C}}\cong\Id_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes(\Id_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\Id_{\mathbf{C}}),

which are subject to natural
coherence axioms
(see e.g.
[
ML98
, Section VII]
).
A monoidal category is
strict
if
\upsilon
and
\alpha
are identities.

(2)

A functor
U\colon\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
between monoidal categories that is equipped with a morphism
\epsilon\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\rightarrow UI_{\mathbf{C}}
and a transformation

\lambda\colon U(-)\otimes U(-)\rightarrow U(-\otimes-)

which is natural in both components is
lax monoidal
if it satisfies appropriate associativity and unitarity axioms with respect to
\upsilon
and
\alpha
.
If both
\epsilon
and
\lambda
are isomorphisms (identities) then we say
U
is
strong
(
strict
) monoidal.

{nota}

Given an object
X
of a monoidal category, the
n
th tensor power is denoted

X^{\otimes n}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}X,

and for a functor
G
the expression
GX^{\otimes n}
is parsed as
G(X^{\otimes n})
, as usual.

We now introduce the setting in which our framework for extending dualities applies.
In the simplest sense, the only ingredient is a strong monoidal functor:

Assumption
\thethm
.

We fix strict monoidal categories
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}
with dually equivalent
categories
\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{D}}
; we regard
\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{D}}
as
monoidal categories with tensor products
\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}
dual to the tensor products
\otimes
of
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}
.
Moreover, we fix an adjunction
F\colon\mathbf{D}\dashv\mathbf{C}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU

with unit
\eta
and counit
\varepsilon
(
3.1
),
and we assume that
U
is a strong monoidal functor with
associated natural isomorphisms

\lambda\colon UX\otimes UY\cong U(X\otimes Y)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\epsilon\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\cong UI_{\mathbf{C}}.

We denote the monad dual to the comonad
FU
by
T=\widehat{F}\widehat{U}
, with unit and multiplication

e=\hat{\varepsilon}\colon\Id\rightarrow T\qquad\text{and}\qquad m=\widehat{F}\hat{\eta}\widehat{U}\colon TT\rightarrow T.

Overall, we have the following situation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdash}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{U}}

(3.2)

Remark 1
.

(1)

By Mac Lane’s
Coherence Theorem

[
ML98
, Section VII.2]
, every monoidal
category is equivalent to a strict monoidal category, hence the strictness requirement on
\mathbf{C}
in
Section 3
is without loss of generality.

(2)

The isomorphism
\lambda
can be extended to an isomorphism

\lambda\colon UX_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes UX_{n}\cong U(X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n})

for all finite families of objects
X_{1}\ldots,X_{n}
in
\mathbf{C}
.

(3)

The functor
\widehat{U}\colon\widehat{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{D}}
dual to
U
is a
strong monoidal
left
adjoint of
\widehat{F}
, and the unit and
counit of the adjunction
\widehat{U}\dashv\widehat{F}
are given by
\hat{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\eta}
, respectively. Since
\widehat{U}
is strong monoidal with respect to the isomorphisms

{\hat{\epsilon}}\colon\widehat{I}_{\mathbf{D}}\cong\widehat{U}\widehat{I}_{\mathbf{C}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\hat{\lambda}\colon\widehat{U}X\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\widehat{U}Y\cong\widehat{U}(X\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}Y)\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\hat{\lambda}^{-1},

its right adjoint
\widehat{F}
is lax monoidal
(see e.g.
[
SS02
, p. 17]
) for the natural transformations

(\hat{\epsilon}^{-1})^{-}\colon\widehat{I}_{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow\widehat{F}\widehat{I}_{\mathbf{D}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad((\hat{\eta}\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\hat{\eta})\cdot\hat{\lambda}^{-1})^{-}\colon\widehat{F}X\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\widehat{F}Y\rightarrow\widehat{F}(X\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}Y).

This makes
\widehat{U}\dashv\widehat{F}
a monoidal adjunction and
T=\widehat{F}\widehat{U}
a
monoidal
or
commutative
monad on
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
with monoidal structure
\hat{\delta}\colon TX\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}TY\rightarrow T(X\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}Y)
given as the appropriate composite of the monoidal structures of
\widehat{F}
and
\widehat{U}
.
Note that
\hat{\delta}
also
extends to any arity, that is, for every
n
-tuple of objects
X_{1},\ldots X_{n}
, we obtain a natural transformation

\hat{\delta}\colon TX_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\cdots\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}TX_{n}\to T(X_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\cdots\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}X_{n}).

(4)

The tensor product
{\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}}
of
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
lifts to the
Kleisli category
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{T}
; the lifting maps a pair

(f\colon X\rightarrow TY,g\colon X^{\prime}\to TY^{\prime})
of

\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{T}
-morphisms to the
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{T}
-morphism

\hat{\delta}\cdot(f{\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}}g)\colon X{\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}}X^{\prime}\rightarrow TY{\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}}TY^{\prime}\to T(Y{\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}}Y^{\prime}).

This
makes
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{T}
itself a monoidal
category (see e.g.
[
Sea53
, Prop. 1.2.2]
) with tensor
\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}
, and
the canonical left adjoint
J_{T}\colon\widehat{\mathbf{C}}\to\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{T}
a strict
monoidal functor.

We now show how to extend the duality
\mathbf{C}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
along the adjunction
F\dashv U
to yield a duality between operators.

{defi}

(1)

Let
G\colon{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{B}}
be a functor between
monoidal categories, and let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
. An

(k,n)
-ary
G
-operator
consists of an object

A\in\mathbf{A}
and a morphism

a\colon(GA)^{\otimes k}\rightarrow(GA)^{\otimes n}
of

\mathbf{B}
. A
(k,n)
-ary
G
-operator morphism
from

(A,a)
to
(B,b)
is a morphism
h\colon GA\to GB
of

\mathbf{B}
such that the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(GA)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(GA)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(GB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(GB)^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

The category of
(k,n)
-ary
G
-operators and their
morphisms is denoted by
\op_{G}^{k,n}(\mathbf{A})
.

(2)

A
G
-operator
is a
(k,n)
-ary
G
-operator for some
k
and
n
.

(3)

A
G
-algebra
is a
(k,1)
-ary
G
-operator,
and a
G
-coalgebra
is a
(1,n)
-ary one.

(4)

If
G
is strong monoidal, then a
(k,n)
-ary
G
-operator
(A,a)
is
pure

if there exists a morphism
a^{\prime}\colon A^{\otimes k}\to A^{\otimes n}
such that

a=\big((GA)^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{~\lambda~}G(A^{\otimes k})\xrightarrow{~Ga^{\prime}~}G(A^{\otimes n})\xrightarrow{~\lambda^{-1}~}(GA)^{\otimes n}\big),

where
\lambda
is
given analogously to
Section 3
. An operator morphism
h\colon(A,a)\to(B,b)
is
pure
if there exists a morphism
h^{\prime}\colon A\to B
such that
h=Gh^{\prime}\colon GA\to GB
.

Two cases will be important for intuition: (1) if
G
is a forgetful functor between varieties of algebraic structures, then a
G
-operator on an object
A
is a map preserving only part of the structure on
A
; (2) if
T
is a powerset-like monad on a category of spaces, then an operator for the embedding
J_{T}\colon\widehat{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{T}
on a space
\widehat{A}
is a (continuous)
relation

\widehat{A}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}
.

Theorem 2

(Abstract Extended Duality)
.

The category of
(k,n)
-ary
U
-operators is dually equivalent
to the category of
(n,k)
-ary
J_{T}
-operators:

\op_{U}^{k,n}(\mathbf{C})\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\op_{J_{T}}^{n,k}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}).

Proof 3.1
.

The desired equivalence of categories is given by the functor

\Phi\colon\op_{J_{T}}^{n,k}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}})\rightarrow\op_{U}^{k,n}(\mathbf{C})

(3.3)

which maps a
(n,k)
-ary
J_{T}
-operator

\widehat{a}\colon\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}\to T\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}=\widehat{F}\widehat{U}\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}

to the
(k,n)
-ary
U
-operator

(UA)^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{~\lambda~}UA^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{~a^{-}~}UA^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{~\lambda^{-1}~}(UA)^{\otimes n},

(3.4)

and an operator morphism

\widehat{f}\colon(\widehat{A},\widehat{a})\rightarrow(\widehat{B},\widehat{b})

to
f^{-}\colon UB\rightarrow UA
.

Let us first verify that the definition of
\Phi
makes sense on morphisms, i.e. that
f^{-}
is an operator
morphism from
(B,\lambda^{-1}\cdot b^{-}\cdot\lambda)
to
(A,\lambda^{-1}\cdot a^{-}\cdot\lambda)
.
Unfolding the definitions of composition and monoidal structure in the Kleisli category, we see that
\widehat{f}
is a morphism
\widehat{A}\rightarrow T\widehat{B}=\widehat{F}\widehat{U}\widehat{B}
such that
the following diagram in
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{A}^{\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T\widehat{B})^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\widehat{B})^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}^{\widehat{\otimes}n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{f}^{\widehat{\otimes}k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

(3.5)

The multiplication
m
of the monad is the dual of
F\eta U
, so
the dual diagram of (
3.5
) is precisely the outside
of the following diagram in
\mathbf{C}
, in which all parts except
(\#)
commute by naturality of
\eta,\varepsilon
and
\lambda
, and the triangle equations.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFUB^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUB^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFUB^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUB^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(FUB)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF(UB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UB)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UFUB)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(FUB)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF(UFUB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UFUB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UA)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UA)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUA^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFU(FUB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF(UA)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUA^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(FUB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFUA^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUA^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fb^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF(\eta U)^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\eta U)^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f^{-})^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\#)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f^{-})^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Uf)^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUf^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF(Uf)^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Uf)^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFUf^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUF\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUf^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

To see that
(\#)
also commutes, note that the counit
\varepsilon=\mathrm{id}^{+}
is the
adjoint transpose of the identity, and transposition is natural. So transposing the two paths
from
FUB^{\otimes k}
to
A^{\otimes n}
that form part
(\#)
yields the inner square of the following commutative diagram in
\mathbf{D}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UB^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UB^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UB)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UA)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UA)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(A^{\otimes n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{-})^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({f}^{-})^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}

By pre- and postcomposition of this square with
\lambda
and
\lambda^{-1}
, respectively, and replacing
a^{-}
and
b^{-}
by their respective conjugates
\alpha=\lambda^{-1}\cdot a^{-}\cdot\lambda
and
\beta=\lambda^{-1}\cdot b^{-}\cdot\lambda
this diagram simply becomes the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UB)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UB)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UA)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UA)^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({f}^{-})^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({f}^{-})^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

in
\mathbf{D}
, which is a homomorphism diagram of
(k,n)
-ary
U
-operators.

We have shown that
\Phi
is indeed a functor. Now we verify that it is an equivalence.

The (natural) isomorphisms

\mathbf{D}(UA,UB)\cong\mathbf{C}(FUA,B)\cong\widehat{\mathbf{C}}(\widehat{B},\widehat{F}\widehat{U}\widehat{A})=\widehat{\mathbf{C}}(\widehat{B},T\widehat{A})

given by the duality and the adjunction
F\dashv U
ensure that
this functor is fully faithful.

To see that it is essentially surjective, pick any
U
-operator

c\colon(UC)^{\otimes k}\rightarrow(UC)^{\otimes n}
. We have
to show that it is isomorphic to an operator of the
form (
3.4
). Since the original duality

\mathbf{C}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
is essentially surjective there exists an isomorphism

h\colon C\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\widehat{X}
for some
X\in\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
.
Let
a\colon FU\widehat{X}^{\otimes k}\rightarrow\widehat{X}^{\otimes n}

be the adjoint transpose
of the
\mathbf{D}
-morphism

U\widehat{X}^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{~\lambda^{-1}~}(U\widehat{X})^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{~(Uh^{-1})^{\otimes k}~}(UC)^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{~c~}(UC)^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{~(Uh)^{\otimes n}~}(U\widehat{X})^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{~\lambda~}U\widehat{X}^{\otimes n}.

Then
Uh\colon(UC,c)\rightarrow(U\hat{X},\lambda^{-1}\cdot a^{-}\cdot\lambda)
is a pure operator isomorphism.

Remark 3
.

The definition of
\Phi
(
3.3
) can be slightly generalized to yield a dual correspondence between morphisms
a\colon(UA)^{\otimes k}\to(UB)^{\otimes n}
of
\mathbf{D}
and morphisms
\rho\colon\hat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}\to\hat{T}A^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}
of
\widehat{\mathbf{D}}
: the dual of
a
is given by
\rho=\widehat{h^{+}}
, where
h=\lambda\cdot a\cdot\lambda^{-1}\colon UA^{\otimes k}\to UB^{\otimes n}
.

Our approach to extending dualities is
compositional

on two levels:
first, compositionality of adjunctions allows us to dualize certain operators more precisely;
second, on the object level,
U
-operators themselves admit a compositional structure under morphism composition and tensor product,
leading to
simpler
calculations of dual operators.
We will use the first and second version of compositionality in

Section 4.2

and
4.3
, respectively.

We elaborate on the first point:
Let
\mathbf{E}
be a monoidal category with monoidal adjunctions

F_{1}\colon\mathbf{E}\dashv\mathbf{C}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{2}\colon\mathbf{D}\dashv\mathbf{E}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU_{2}

which
split

F\dashv U
(i.e.
F=F_{1}F_{2}
,
U=U_{2}U_{1}
), and
suppose that the monoidal structure of
U
is given by

\lambda=U_{2}\lambda_{1}\cdot\lambda_{2}U_{1}
.
The compositionality of adjunctions leads to
the following lifting property, applying to both operators
and operator morphisms by setting

A=B
and
k=n=1
, respectively, in the following proposition.
Here we say that, for a monoidal functor
G
, a morphism
f\colon(GX)^{\otimes i}\rightarrow(GY)^{\otimes j}

lifts along
G
, if there exists a morphism
g\colon X^{\otimes i}\rightarrow Y^{\otimes j}
with
f=\lambda^{-1}\cdot Gg\cdot\lambda
.
The morphism
g
is called a
lifting
of
f
.

Proposition 4
.

Let
a\colon(UA)^{\otimes k}\rightarrow(UB)^{\otimes n}
be a
morphism in
\mathbf{D}
dual to
\rho\colon\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}
in
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
as in
3
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

a
lifts along
U_{2}
, that is,

a=\lambda_{2}^{-1}\cdot U_{2}b\cdot\lambda_{2}\qquad\text{for some $b\colon(U_{1}A)^{\otimes k}\rightarrow(U_{1}B)^{\otimes n}$};

(2)

\rho
factorizes through the monad morphism

\widehat{F}_{1}\hat{\varepsilon}_{2}\widehat{U}_{1}\colon T_{1}\rightarrow T
(where
T_{1}=\widehat{F}_{1}\widehat{U}_{1}
), that is,

\rho=\widehat{F}_{1}\hat{\varepsilon}_{2}\widehat{U}_{1}\cdot\sigma\qquad\text{for some $\sigma\colon\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}\rightarrow T_{1}\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}$}.

Proof 3.2
.

The dual of the morphism
a\colon(U_{2}U_{1}A)^{\otimes k}\rightarrow(U_{2}U_{1}B)^{\otimes n}
under the abstract extended duality is given by

\rho=\widehat{h^{+}}\colon\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}=\widehat{F}_{1}\widehat{F}_{2}\widehat{U}_{2}\widehat{U}_{1}\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k},

where
h
is the unique morphism making the outside of the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}U_{1}A^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}U_{1}B^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}(U_{1}A)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}(U_{1}B)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{2}U_{1}A)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{2}U_{1}B)^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}\lambda_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{2}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

We denote the transposition isomorphisms of the adjunctions
F_{i}\dashv U_{i}
by

(-)^{\flat_{i}}\colon\mathbf{C}(F_{i}X,Y)\rightleftarrows\mathbf{D}(X,U_{i}Y)\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu(-)^{\sharp_{i}}.

Now assume that

\widehat{g^{\sharp_{1}}}\colon\widehat{B}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}n}\rightarrow T_{1}\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}=\widehat{F}_{1}\widehat{U}_{1}\widehat{A}^{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}k}
is a morphism in
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}

such that we have a factorization
\widehat{h^{+}}=\widehat{F}_{1}\hat{\varepsilon}_{2}\widehat{U}_{1}\cdot\widehat{g^{\sharp_{1}}}
.
Under duality this is equivalent to

h^{+}=g^{\sharp_{1}}\cdot F_{1}\varepsilon_{2}U_{1}\,\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\,h^{\sharp_{2}}=(h^{+})^{\flat_{1}}=g\cdot\varepsilon_{2}U_{1}\,\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\,h=(h^{\sharp_{2}})^{\flat_{2}}=U_{2}g\cdot(\varepsilon_{2}U_{1})^{\flat_{2}}=U_{2}g

using naturality of the isomorphisms
\flat_{1},\flat_{2}
.
The dual of the
J_{T_{1}}
-operator
\widehat{g^{\sharp_{1}}}
under the abstract extended duality along the adjunction
F_{1}\dashv U_{1}
is the
U_{1}
-operator
b=\lambda_{1}^{-1}\cdot g\cdot\lambda_{1}
.
Therefore the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}U_{1}A^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}U_{1}B^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}(U_{1}A)^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}(U_{1}B)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{2}U_{1}A^{\otimes k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{2}U_{1})B^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}g\,=\,h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}\lambda_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{2}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

This is equivalent to
a
admitting the lifting
b
, that is,
a=\lambda_{2}^{-1}\cdot U_{2}b\cdot\lambda_{2}
.

Remark 5
.

(1)

A special case of
4

proves that extended dualities preserve purity: splitting

F\dashv U
into
F_{1}=\Id\dashv\Id=U_{1}
and

F_{2}=F\dashv U=U_{2}
, we see that a
U
-operator (or
operator morphism)
a
is pure iff its dual
f
is pure as a
Kleisli morphism, that is, it factorizes through the unit
\hat{\varepsilon}
of
the monad
T
.

(2)

The right adjoint
U_{2}
often is faithful and in this case

\widehat{F}_{1}\hat{\varepsilon}_{2}\widehat{U}_{1}
is monic, that is,
T_{1}

is a submonad of
T
: indeed, faithfulness of
U_{2}
is equivalent to
having an epic counit
\varepsilon_{2}
, hence

\hat{\varepsilon}_{2}\widehat{U}_{1}
is monic, and the right adjoint

\hat{F_{1}}
preserves monos. In particular, if
T
is
‘powerset-like’, then
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{T}
is a category of relations,
and we think of
U
-operators (or operator morphisms) of the form

a=\lambda_{2}^{-1}\cdot U_{2}b\cdot\lambda_{2}
as
dualizing to ‘more functional’ relations. This idea is
illustrated by the examples in
Section 4.2
.

The compositionality on the level of
U
-operators manifests itself
as follows:

Proposition 6
.

Let
h,g\colon UA\rightarrow UA
be
U
-operators with respective duals
\rho,\sigma\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}
.
On objects the abstract extended duality of
2
preserves

(1)

Tensor products of operators:

h\otimes g\colon UA\otimes UA\rightarrow UA\otimes UA\quad\text{is dual to}\quad\hat{\delta}\cdot(\rho\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\sigma)\colon\widehat{A}\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\widehat{A}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}T\widehat{A}\rightarrow T(\widehat{A}\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\widehat{A}).

(2)

Composition of operators:

g\cdot h\colon UA\rightarrow UA\rightarrow UA\quad\text{is dual to}\quad m\cdot T\rho\cdot\sigma\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}\rightarrow TT\widehat{A}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}.

(3)

Identity operators:

\mathrm{id}_{UA}\colon UA\rightarrow UA\quad\text{is dual to}\quad e_{\widehat{A}}\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow T\widehat{A}.

Proof 3.3
.

(1)

Under the extended duality the operator
h\otimes g
is
mapped to
\widehat{\alpha^{+}}
, the dual of the adjoint
transpose
\alpha^{+}
of the conjugate
\alpha
of
h\otimes g
:

\alpha=\big(U(A\otimes A)\xrightarrow{~\lambda^{-1}~}UA\otimes UA\xrightarrow{~h\otimes g~}UA\otimes UA\xrightarrow{~\lambda~}U(A\otimes A)\big).

We now prove that the following diagram commutes, where
\delta
is the comonoidal structure of
FU
dual to the monoidal structure
\hat{\delta}
of
T
from
1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(A\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UA\otimes UA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UFUA\otimes UFUA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(UA\otimes UA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUA\otimes FUA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(FUA\otimes FUA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(A\otimes A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\eta\otimes\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(h\otimes g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Uh^{+}\otimes Ug^{+})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{+}\otimes g^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(h^{+}\otimes g^{+})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

Its outside commutes using the definition of the adjoint transpose
\alpha^{+}
, and the upper part also commutes by adjoint transposition. The right-hand and lower parts commute by naturality of
\lambda
and
\varepsilon
, respectively. The middle part commutes trivially by the definition of
\delta
.
Thus, the left-hand triangle commutes.
This shows that, under the duality
\mathbf{C}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
, the dual of
\alpha^{+}
is equal to the dual of
(h^{+}\otimes g^{+})\cdot{\delta}
, which in turn is given by
\hat{\delta}\cdot(\rho\mathbin{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.0pt}\smash{\otimes}}}\sigma)
.

(2)

Similarly, the adjoint transpose of
g\cdot h
is equal to
g^{+}\cdot FUh^{+}\cdot F\eta U
whose dual is given by
m\cdot T\rho\cdot\sigma
.

(3)

The adjoint transpose of
\mathrm{id}_{UA}
is
\varepsilon_{A}
, whose dual is the unit
\hat{\varepsilon}_{\hat{A}}=e_{\hat{A}}
of
T
.

4.
Example: Extended Priestley Duality

As a first application of our adjoint framework, we investigate the
classical Priestley duality (
Section 2
) and derive a
generalized version of Goldblatt’s duality
[
Gol89
]
between
distributive lattices with operators and relational Priestley
spaces. We instantiate (
3.2
) to the following categories
and functors, which we will subsequently explain in detail:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(4.1)

Categories

The upper duality is Hofman-Mislove-Stralka
duality
[
HMS74
]
between the category of join-semilattices with a
bottom element and the category of Stone semilattices (i.e. topological join-semilattices with
a bottom element whose underlying topological space is a Stone space) and continuous
semilattice homomorphisms. The duality maps a join-semilattice
J
to the Stone semilattice

\mathbf{JSL}(J,2)
of semilattice homomorphisms into the two-element semilattice, topologized by
pointwise convergence. Equivalently,
\mathbf{JSL}(J,2)
is the space
\idl(J)
of
ideals

(downward closed and upward directed subsets) of
J
, ordered by reverse inclusion, with topology generated by the subbasic open sets
\sigma(j)=\{I\in\idl(J)\mid j\in I\}
and their complements for
j\in J
.
In the other direction, a Stone semilattice
X
is mapped to its semilattice
\mathbf{StoneJSL}(X,2)
of clopen ideals, ordered by inclusion.

Functors

The functor

U\colon\mathbf{DL}\rightarrow\mathbf{JSL}
is the obvious forgetful
functor. Its left adjoint
F\colon\mathbf{JSL}\rightarrow\mathbf{DL}
maps a
join-semilattice
J
to the set
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{fg}}^{\partial}(J)

of finitely generated upsets of
J
, ordered by reverse inclusion.
The dual right adjoint
\widehat{F}
of
F
is the
forgetful functor mapping a Stone semilattice to its underlying Priestley space.
Indeed, since
F\dashv U
, we compute for the carrier set
|X|
of a Stone semilattice
X
that

|\widehat{F}X|=|\widehat{F\widehat{X}}|\cong|\mathbf{DL}(F\widehat{X},2)|\cong|\mathbf{JSL}(\widehat{X},U2)|=|\mathbf{JSL}(\widehat{X},2)|\cong|X|,

and this bijection is a homeomorphism.

The left adjoint
\widehat{U}\colon\mathbf{Priest}\rightarrow\mathbf{StoneJSL}
maps a Priestley
space
X
to the Stone join-semilattice

\widehat{U\widehat{X}}=\mathbf{JSL}(U(\mathbf{Priest}(X,2)),2)\cong\idl(\cl_{\uparrow}X)

of ideals of clopen upsets
of
X
. This space is isomorphic to the
(downset) Vietoris
hyperspace

\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X
of
X
carried by the set of
closed downsets of
X
. The isomorphism is given by

\displaystyle\idl(\cl_{\uparrow}X)\qquad

\displaystyle\cong\qquad\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X

\displaystyle I\qquad

\displaystyle\mapsto\qquad\bigcap_{U\in I}X\setminus U

\displaystyle\{U\in\cl_{\uparrow}X\mid C\subseteq X\setminus U\}\qquad

\displaystyle\mapsfrom\qquad C.

The topology of pointwise convergence on
\mathbf{JSL}(U(\mathbf{Priest}(X,2)),2)

translates to the
hit-or-miss topology
on
\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X

generated by the subbasic open sets

\{A\subseteq X\text{ closed }\mid A\cap U\neq\emptyset\}\quad{\text{for}}\quad U\in\cl_{\uparrow}X

and their complements.
Note that for a Stone space
X
, the Stone join-semilattice
\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X
is the
free Stone join-semilattice

on
X
,
as observed by Johnstone
[
Joh82
, Sec. 4.8]
.
The monad induced by the adjunction is the
(downset) Vietoris monad
;
its unit

e\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X
is given by

x\mapsto{\downarrow}x
and multiplication is given by union
[
HN15
]
.
The monad
\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}
restricts to the Vietoris monad
\mathbb{V}
on the category
\mathbf{Stone}
of Stone spaces.

The duality of modal algebras and coalgebras for the Vietoris construction, going back to Esakia
[
Esa74
]
, has often been rediscovered and extended since, see e.g.
[
KKV04
,
VV14
]
; for recent accounts with detailed computations regarding the dualities of
FU
and
\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}
see Bezhanishvili et al.
[
BHM23
]
or the textbook by Gehrke and van Gool
[
GvG24
, Section 6.4]
.

Remark 7

(Continuous Relations)
.

Continuous maps in
\mathbf{Priest}
of the form

\rho\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}Y

are known in the literature under a variety of names; we call them as
Priestley relations
as in
[
CLP91
,
Gol89
]
, or
Stone relations
if
X,Y
are Stone spaces. We write
x\mathbin{\rho}y
for
y\in\rho(x)
, and sometimes identify
\rho
with a subset of
X\times Y
. Let us note that some authors (e.g.
[
RS09
]
) call a relation
R\subseteq X\times Y
between topological spaces
continuous
if it is closed as a subspace of
X\times Y
.
Every Priestley relation is continuous. The converse generally fails: for instance, consider any non-discrete Stone space
X
and let
C\subseteq X
be a subset that is closed but not open. The relation
C\times 1\subseteq X\times 1
is closed, but the corresponding map
\rho\colon X\to\mathbb{V}1
(given by
\rho(x)=1
if
x\in C
and
\rho(x)=\emptyset
otherwise) is not continuous because
\rho^{-1}[1]=C
is not open.

Monoidal Structure

The category
\mathbf{JSL}
of
join-semilattices has a tensor product
\otimes
that represents
join-bilinear
maps, that is, maps
J\times J^{\prime}\to K
between join-semilattices preserving finite joins in each argument:

\mathrm{Bilin}(J\times J^{\prime},K)\;\cong\;\mathbf{JSL}(J\otimes J^{\prime},K).

Join-bilinear maps
J\times J^{\prime}\to K
and their corresponding
\mathbf{JSL}
-morphisms
J\otimes J^{\prime}\to K
are often tacitly identified. The tensor product
\otimes
makes
\mathbf{JSL}
a monoidal category with unit
2
,
i.e.
2\otimes J\cong J
. The standard presentation of
J\otimes J^{\prime}
as a join-semilattice is given
by the generators
\{j\otimes j^{\prime}\mid j\in J,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\}

with equations

j_{1}\otimes 0=0\otimes j^{\prime}_{1}=0,\quad(j_{1}\lor j_{2})\otimes j^{\prime}=j_{1}\otimes j^{\prime}\lor j_{2}\otimes j^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad j\otimes(j^{\prime}_{1}\lor j^{\prime}_{2})=j\otimes j^{\prime}_{1}\lor j\otimes j^{\prime}_{2}

ranging over
j_{1},j_{2}\in J
and
j^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{2}\in J^{\prime}
.
We call (the equivalence class of) a generating element
j\otimes j^{\prime}
a
pure tensor
.
If
D,D^{\prime}
are bounded distributive lattices then so is
UD\otimes UD^{\prime}

[
Fra76
]
,
with meet given on pure tensors by
(d\otimes d^{\prime})\land(e\otimes e^{\prime})=(d\land e)\otimes(d^{\prime}\land e^{\prime})
.
Moreover, the lattice
UD\otimes UD^{\prime}
is the coproduct of
D,D^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{DL}
:
the coproduct injections are given by

\iota(d)=d\otimes 1^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\iota^{\prime}(d^{\prime})=1\otimes d^{\prime}

for
d\in D,d^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}
, and the copairing of lattice homomorphisms
f\colon D\rightarrow E,f^{\prime}\colon D^{\prime}\rightarrow E
is given by the extension of the join-bilinear map

\land\cdot(f\times f^{\prime})\colon D\times D^{\prime}\rightarrow E,\qquad(d,d^{\prime})\mapsto f(d)\land f(d^{\prime}).

Taking coproducts yields a monoidal structure on
\mathbf{DL}
, and since
U(D+D^{\prime})=UD\otimes UD^{\prime}
, the functor
U
is strict monoidal.

Lemma 8
.

The dual monoidal structure
\hat{\delta}
of
\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}
is given by product:

\hat{\delta}\colon\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X\times\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}Y\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(X\times Y),\qquad(C,D)\mapsto C\times D.

Proof 4.1
.

Let
C\in\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X,D\in\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}Y
be closed downsets.
We first represent them by their respective ideals
I_{C},I_{D}
of
\cl_{\uparrow}X
and
\cl_{\uparrow}Y
, which are equivalently
\mathbf{JSL}
-morphisms

c\colon U\cl_{\uparrow}X\rightarrow U2,\,d\colon U\cl_{\uparrow}Y\rightarrow U2.

The map
\hat{\delta}
is the given as the dual

\mathbf{DL}(FU\cl_{\uparrow}X+FU\cl_{\uparrow}Y,2)\rightarrow\mathbf{DL}(FU(\cl_{\uparrow}X+\cl_{\uparrow}Y),2)

of the comonoidal structure
FU(A+B)\rightarrow FUA+FUB

mapping
[c^{+},d^{+}]
to the prime filter that is the transpose of

\displaystyle U[c^{+},d^{+}]\cdot(\eta\otimes\eta)\colon

\displaystyle U(\cl_{\uparrow}X+\cl_{\uparrow}Y)

\displaystyle\cong\,

\displaystyle U(\cl_{\uparrow}X)\otimes U(\cl_{\uparrow}Y)\rightarrow UFU\cl_{\uparrow}X\otimes UFU\cl_{\uparrow}Y

\displaystyle\cong\,

\displaystyle U(FU\cl_{\uparrow}X+FU\cl_{\uparrow}Y)\rightarrow U2.

The latter map sends a pure tensor
A\otimes B\in U(\cl_{\uparrow}X+\cl_{\uparrow}Y)
to its ‘product’
c(A)\land d(B)
.
Therefore the closed set
\hat{\delta}(C,D)
corresponding to

U[c^{+},d^{+}]\cdot(\eta\otimes\eta)
contains a pair
(x,y)
iff
x\in C
and
y\in D
.

Expanding
Section 3
, the category
\op_{J_{\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}}}^{n,k}(\mathbf{Priest})
is given as follows:

{defi}

A (
(n,k)
-ary
)
relational Priestley space
consists of a
carrier Priestley space
X
and a Priestley relation

\rho\colon X^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X^{k}
. A
relational
morphism
from a relational Priestley space
(X,\rho)
to a
relational Priestley space

(X^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
is given by a Priestley relation

\beta\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}Y
such that, for all

\mathbf{x}\in X^{n}
,
\mathbf{y}\in X^{k}
, and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in X^{\prime k}
,

\mathbf{x}\mathrel{\rho}\mathbf{y}\land(\forall i\colon y_{i}\mathrel{\beta}y_{i}^{\prime})\implies\exists\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\colon(\forall i\colon x_{i}\mathrel{\beta}x_{i}^{\prime})\land\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mathrel{\rho^{\prime}}\mathbf{y}^{\prime},

and, for all
\mathbf{x}\in X^{n}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in X^{\prime n}
, and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in X^{\prime k}
,

(\forall i\colon x_{i}\mathrel{\beta}x_{i}^{\prime})\land\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mathrel{\rho^{\prime}}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\implies\exists\mathbf{y}\colon\mathbf{x}\mathrel{\rho}\mathbf{y}\land(\forall i\colon y_{i}\mathrel{\beta}y_{i}^{\prime}).

We denote by
\op_{J_{\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}}}^{n,k}(\mathbf{Priest})
the category of
(n,k)
-ary relational Priestley spaces and relational morphisms.

Then
2
instantiates to the following result:

Theorem 9

(Extended Priestley duality)
.

The category of
(k,n)
-ary

U
-operators of distributive lattices is dually equivalent to the category of
(n,k)
-ary relational Priestley spaces and relational
morphisms:

\op_{U}^{k,n}(\mathbf{DL})\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\op_{J_{\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}}}^{n,k}(\mathbf{Priest}).

By setting
n=1
and restricting the operator morphisms on both sides to be pure (
5
(1)
),
we recover Goldblatt’s duality
[
Gol89
]
. In the latter work,
pure relational morphisms are called
bounded morphisms
and
n
-ary
U
-algebras
(UD)^{\otimes n}\to UD
in
\mathbf{JSL}
are called
n
-ary
join-hemimorphisms
.

Corollary 10

(Goldblatt duality)
.

The category of distributive lattices with
n
-ary join-hemimorphisms, and pure morphisms between them, is dually equivalent
to the category of
(1,n)
-relational Priestley spaces and bounded morphisms.

4.1.
Deriving Concrete Formulas

We proceed to show how an order-enriched extension of our adjoint framework can be used to
methodically derive concrete (i.e. element-based)
formulas for the dual join operator of a continuous relation and vice versa.
Let us first observe that all involved
categories are
order-enriched
if we equip the homsets
with the usual pointwise order on functions.
Moreover, from the definitions it is clear that the
transposing isomorphisms of the adjunction
F\dashv U
and the
duality
\mathbf{DL}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{Priest}
are order-isomorphisms.

Second, we can represent an element
\hat{x}
of a Priestley space
\widehat{X}
as a continuous function
\hat{x}\in\mathbf{Priest}(1,\widehat{X})
;
on the lattice side, elements
j
of a join-semilattice
J
correspond bijectively to
\mathbf{JSL}
-morphisms
j\in\mathbf{JSL}(2,J)
, using that
2
is the free semilattice on a single generator.

For the rest of this subsection let us fix a
U
-algebra
h
and its dual Priestley relation
\rho
:

h\colon(UX)^{\otimes n}\rightarrow UX\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho\colon\widehat{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{X}^{n}.

We first show how to express
\rho
in terms of
h
. Viewing
\rho
as a relation from
\widehat{X}
to
\widehat{X}^{n}
(
7
), two elements
\hat{x}\in\widehat{X},\mathbf{\hat{x}}\in\widehat{X}^{n}
are related by
\rho
(i.e.
\hat{x}\mathrel{\rho}\mathbf{\hat{x}}
) iff the inequality

e(\mathbf{\hat{x}})={\downarrow}{\mathbf{\hat{x}}}\leq\rho(\hat{x})
holds,
equivalently, iff the left diagram below
commutes laxly as indicated:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{X}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(UX)^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U2)^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

\geq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ux}

\geq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes_{i}Ux_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\hat{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\hat{x}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}

The duals of
\hat{x},\hat{x}_{i}
are
\mathbf{DL}
morphisms

x,x_{i}\colon X\rightarrow 2
.
Under duality and transposition the left diagram
corresponds to the right diagram where
\nabla
is the codiagonal
given by
n
-fold conjunction, i.e. it maps

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}
to
\A_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}
.
Writing
F_{z}=z^{-1}(1)
for the prime filter corresponding to a morphism
z\in\mathbf{DL}(X,2)
the right diagram yields Goldblatt’s formula
[
Gol89
, p. 186]

for the dual Priestley relation of an algebra
h
: we have

\hat{x}\mathrel{\rho}\mathbf{\hat{x}}\qquad\text{iff}\qquad h[\prod_{i}F_{x_{i}}]\subseteq F_{x}.

Conversely, to express
h
in terms of
\rho
, it suffices to describe

h(\mathbf{x})
for a pure tensor
\mathbf{x}\in(UX)^{\otimes n}
by the universal property of the tensor product.
We factorize

\mathbf{x}=\bigotimes_{i}x_{i}\cdot\nabla^{-1}\colon U2\cong(U2)^{\otimes n}\rightarrow(UX)^{\otimes n}

to see that the element
h(\mathbf{x})
corresponds to the following morphism
representing an element of the join-semilattice
UX
:

h\cdot\bigotimes_{i}x_{i}\cdot\nabla^{-1}\colon U2\cong(U2)^{\otimes n}\rightarrow(UX)^{\otimes n}\rightarrow UX.

Its dual is the characteristic function

\widehat{X}\xrightarrow{~\rho~}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{X}^{n}\xrightarrow{~\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\prod_{i}C_{i})~}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}1)^{n}\xrightarrow{~\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\hat{\delta}~}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}1^{n}\xrightarrow{~m~}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}1^{n}\xrightarrow{~\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\Delta^{-1}~}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}1=2,

where
C_{i}=\widehat{x_{i}^{+}}
is the clopen upset of
\widehat{X}
dual to

x_{i}\in\mathbf{JSL}(U2,UX)\;\cong\;\mathbf{DL}(FU2,X)\;\cong\;\mathbf{Priest}(\widehat{X},\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}1)\;\cong\;\mathbf{Priest}(\widehat{X},2).

This shows that
h(\mathbf{x})\in X\;\cong\;\cl_{\uparrow}\widehat{X}
corresponds to the clopen upset

h(\mathbf{x})=\{a\in\widehat{X}\mid\exists(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\rho(a)\colon\forall i\colon b_{i}\in C_{i}=\widehat{x_{i}^{+}}\}\in\cl_{\uparrow}(\widehat{X}),

so we derived Goldblatt’s formula
[
Gol89
, p. 184]
for the dual algebra of a relation
\rho
.

4.2.
Functional Properties of Priestley Relations.

As a further application of the adjoint framework to extended Stone duality, we show how to recover the characterization of those operators on distributive lattices whose dual Priestley relation is a partial function or a total relation, respectively.
As outlined in
5
, we achieve this by considering
suitable splittings of the adjunction
F\colon\mathbf{JSL}\dashv\mathbf{DL}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU
to obtain submonads of
\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}
on which we instantiate
4
.

Partial Functions.

We split the adjunction
F\dashv U
into

F_{1}\colon\mathbf{DL}_{0}\dashv\mathbf{DL}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{2}\colon\mathbf{JSL}\dashv\mathbf{DL}_{0}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU_{2},

where
\mathbf{DL}_{0}
is the
category of distributive lattices that are only bounded from below,
and
U_{1},U_{2}
are forgetful functors.
The tensor product of objects of
\mathbf{DL}_{0}
is given by the tensor product of their underlying join-semilattices.
The left adjoint
F_{1}
adds a top element
to a lattice in
\mathbf{DL}_{0}
.

Lemma 11
.

The submonad
T_{1}
on
\mathbf{Priest}
dual to the adjunction
F_{1}\dashv U_{1}
is given by the partial function monad

T_{1}X=X+\{\emptyset\}.

Proof 4.2
.

The submonad

T_{1}=\widehat{F}_{1}\widehat{U}_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}
on
\mathbf{Priest}
is given by

\widehat{F}_{1}\widehat{U}_{1}\widehat{D}\;\cong\;\widehat{F_{1}U_{1}D}\;\cong\;\mathbf{DL}(F_{1}U_{1}D,2)\;\cong\;\mathbf{DL}_{0}(U_{1}D,U_{1}2).

Every morphism
f\in\mathbf{DL}_{0}(U_{1}D,U_{1}2)
either satisfies
f(\top)=\top
, in
which case
f\in\widehat{D}
is prime; or
f(\top)=\bot
, but then

f
is the constant zero map
\bot!\colon U_{1}D\rightarrow U_{1}2
.
The map
\bot!
is the bottom element in the pointwise ordering of

\mathbf{DL}_{0}(U_{1}D,U_{1}2)
, so the monad
\widehat{F}_{1}\widehat{U}_{1}
just freely adjoins a bottom
element to a Priestley space.

In particular, the dual category of
\mathbf{DL}_{0}
is readily
seen to be equivalent to
\mathbf{Priest}_{0}
, the category of Priestley
spaces with a bottom element, and bottom-preserving continuous
monotone maps. A Kleisli morphism

\rho\colon X\rightarrow T_{1}X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X
is a

partial continuous function
, and a
U
-operator lifts along
U_{2}
iff it preserves non-empty meets.

4
thus recovers the following result (for the unary version see
[
Hal58
,
HN15
]
).

Corollary 12
.

The dual Priestley relation of a
U
-operator is a partial function iff the operator preserves non-empty meets.

Total Relations.

We split the adjunction
F\dashv U
into

F^{\prime}_{1}\colon\mathbf{JSL}_{1}\dashv\mathbf{DL}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU_{1}^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F^{\prime}_{2}\colon\mathbf{JSL}\dashv\mathbf{JSL}_{1}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU^{\prime}_{2},

where
\mathbf{JSL}_{1}
is the category
of join-semilattices with both a bottom and top element with morphisms
preserving joins, bottom, and top. The right adjoints
U^{\prime}_{1},U^{\prime}_{2}
are forgetful functors,
and the tensor product of objects of
\mathbf{JSL}_{1}
is given by the tensor product of their underlying join-semilattices.
The left adjoint
F^{\prime}_{1}
maps
J\in\mathbf{JSL}_{1}
to the distributive
lattice
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{fg}+}^{\partial}J
of
non-empty

finitely generated upsets of
J
, ordered by reverse inclusion.

Lemma 13
.

The submonad
T^{\prime}_{1}
on
\mathbf{Priest}
dual to the adjunction
F^{\prime}_{1}\dashv U^{\prime}_{1}
is given by the non-empty Vietoris monad

T^{\prime}_{1}X=\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}^{+}X.

Proof 4.3
.

We have

\widehat{F}^{\prime}_{1}\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}\widehat{D}\;\cong\;\mathbf{DL}(F^{\prime}_{1}U^{\prime}_{1}D,2)\;\cong\;\mathbf{JSL}_{1}(U^{\prime}_{1}D,U^{\prime}_{1}2).

The only ideal
f\in\mathbf{JSL}(UD,U2)
that is not an
element of
\mathbf{JSL}_{1}(U^{\prime}_{1}D,U^{\prime}_{1}2)

is the trivial ideal
\bot!\colon d\longmapsto\bot
: if
f(\top)\neq\top
then
f(\top)=\bot
, so
f
maps all elements to
\bot
by monotonicity.. The trivial ideal corresponds to the empty set
\emptyset\in\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{D}
.
Thus
T^{\prime}_{1}X=\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}X\setminus\{\emptyset\}
.

Kleisli morphisms
X\rightarrow T_{1}^{\prime}Y
therefore are simply
total
Priestley relations. Moreover, a
U
-operator lifts along
U_{2}^{\prime}
iff it preserves the top element.
4
then yields the following result (for the unary version see
[
Hal58
,
HN15
]
).

Corollary 14
.

The dual Priestley relation of a
U
-operator is a total relation iff the operator preserves the top element.

4.3.
Equational Properties of Operators

In
Section 4.2
we have shown how to use factorizations of the adjunction
F\dashv U
to obtain more precise dualities for operators with additional properties.
However, spelling out and computing the factorizing adjunctions and their dual monads for the desired operator property individually is tedious, and there often is a simpler method:
If an operator property can be described
equationally
,
we can use the fact that inequations of
operators translate to inequations of their dual relations to obtain additional information under dualization.

The results we recover in this section are usually associated with modal correspondence theory
[
vB77
,
GT75
,
BdRV01
]
. Note that our proofs neither use first-order logic nor canonical frames: after having found a suitable encoding of order-theoretic properties as operator (in-)equations they are mere instantiations of the abstract results from
Section 3
.

Proposition 15
.

Let
h\colon UA\rightarrow UA
be an operator on a bounded distributive lattice
A
with dual Priestley relation
\rho\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}
.
The following correspondences hold:

(1)

\rho
is reflexive iff
\forall a\in A\colon a\leq h(a)
.

(2)

\rho
is symmetric iff
\forall a,b\in A\colon a\land h(b)\leq h(h(a)\land b)
.

(3)

\rho
is euclidean iff
\forall a,b\in A\colon h(a)\land h(b)\leq h(a\land h(b))
.

(4)

\rho
is transitive iff
\forall a,b\in A\colon h(a)\land h(b)\geq h(a\land h(b))
.

(5)

\rho
is total iff
h(\top)=\top
.

(6)

\rho
is empty iff
h(\top)=\bot
.

The proof follows a simple pattern: (a) translate the inequation into an inequation between operators, (b) dualize the inequation between operators to an inequation between Priestley relations and (c) convert the relational inequality into the corresponding first-order property.

Proof 4.4
.

First we dualize some canonical operators whose combinations we can then dualize using
6
.
Recall from
6
(3)
that the identity operator
\mathrm{id}_{UA}\colon UA\rightarrow UA
dualizes to the unit
e\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}
.
The conjunction operator
\land\colon UA\otimes UA\rightarrow UA
is equal to the conjugate

\lambda\cdot U\nabla\cdot\lambda^{-1}\colon UA\otimes UA\cong U(A+A)\rightarrow UA\cong UA

and thus dualizes to the diagonal function
\Delta\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\widehat{A}\times\widehat{A}
.

Now let
h\colon UA\rightarrow UA
be a unary operator with dual Priestley relation
\rho\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}
.

(1)

The condition
\forall a\in A\colon a\leq h(a)
is equivalent to the operator inequality
\mathrm{id}_{UA}\leq h
.
The dual inequality is given by
e\leq\rho\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}
, that is,
\forall x\in\widehat{A}\colon\{x\}\subseteq\rho(x)
, which states precisely that
\rho
is reflexive.

(2)

The condition
\forall a,b\in A\colon a\land h(b)\leq h(h(a)\land b)
is equivalent to
\land\cdot(\mathrm{id}\otimes h)\leq h\cdot\land\cdot(h\otimes\mathrm{id})
.
The left side dualizes to

\hat{\delta}\cdot(e\times\rho)\cdot\Delta\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\widehat{A}\times\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}\times\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\widehat{A}\times\widehat{A})

which is given by
x\mapsto\{(x,y)\mid y\in\rho(x)\}
,
while the right side dualizes to

m\cdot\hat{\delta}\cdot\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\rho\times e)\cdot\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\Delta\cdot\rho\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\widehat{A}\times\widehat{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}\times\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\widehat{A}\times\widehat{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\widehat{A}\times\widehat{A})

which is given by
x\mapsto\{(z,y)\mid y\in\rho(x),z\in\rho(y)\}
.
On elements this inequality of relations reads

\forall x,y,z\in\widehat{A}\colon y\in\rho(x)\Rightarrow x\in\rho(y),

which states precisely that
\rho
is symmetric.

(3), (4)

The proof is analogous to part (2) and left as an exercise to the reader.

(5)

Here we have to use two auxiliary operators that exist on every Boolean algebra:
the ‘bottom’ operator

z\colon UA\rightarrow UA,\quad x\mapsto\bot\qquad\text{whose dual is the empty relation}\qquad\zeta\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A},\quad x\mapsto\emptyset.

and the ‘top’ operator

t\colon UA\rightarrow UA,\quad x\mapsto\begin{cases}\bot&\text{if }x=\bot\\
\top&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad\text{whose dual is}\qquad\tau\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A},\quad x\mapsto\widehat{A}.

The equation
h(\top)=\top
holds iff the equation
h\cdot t=t\colon UA\rightarrow UA
of operators holds.
The left side dualizes to

m\cdot\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\tau\cdot\rho\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A},\quad x\mapsto\bigcup_{y\in\rho(x)}\tau(y)=\begin{cases}\widehat{A}&\text{if $\rho(x)$ is non-empty},\\
\emptyset&\text{else}.\end{cases}

The right side is simply
\tau\colon\widehat{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\widehat{A},x\mapsto\widehat{A}
,
and these relations are equal iff
\rho
is total, that is,
\rho(x)
is non-empty for every
x
.

(3)

One has
h(\top)=\bot
iff the operator equation
h=z
holds.
Its dual equation
\rho=\zeta
simply states that
\rho(x)
is empty for every
x
.

Remark 16
.

The axioms (1)–(4) considered in
15
correspond to the classical
modal axioms (T), (B), (D) and (5). Usually, axioms (2)–(4) are phrased differently by
using
Boolean
modal logic: (2) is equivalent to

\forall a\colon a\leq\neg h(\neg h(a))
, (3) to

\forall a\colon h(a)\leq\neg h(\neg h(a))
and (4) to
h(h(a))\leq h(a)
. We show that
axiom (2) is equivalent to (B) and leave the rest as an easy exercise for the reader.

Suppose that (2) holds in a Boolean algebra
B
, that is, it satisfies

\forall a,b\in B\colon a\land h(b)\leq h(h(a)\land b)
. We prove that it
satisfies
\forall a\in B\colon a\leq\neg h(\neg h(a))
. Note that in a Boolean algebra

p\leq q
is equivalent to
p\land\neg q\leq\bot
. Hence, we have

a\land\neg\neg h(\neg h(a))=a\land h(\neg h(a))\leq h(h(a)\land\neg h(a))\leq h(\bot)=\bot,

where we use (2) instantiated with
b=\neg h(a)
in the middle inequality. So
B
satisfies (B).
For the converse, suppose that
B
satisfies
\forall a\colon a\leq\neg h(\neg h(a))
. We show that it satisfies
\forall a,b\colon a\land h(b)\leq h(h(a)\land b)
:

\displaystyle(a\land h(b))\land\neg h(h(a)\land b)\leq\neg h(\neg h(a))\land h(b)\land\neg h(h(a)\land b)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle h(b)\land\neg(h(\neg h(a))\lor h(h(a)\land b))=h(b)\land\neg h(\neg h(a)\lor(h(a)\land b))

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle h(b)\land\neg h(\neg h(a)\lor b)=h(b)\land\neg h(\neg h(a))\land\neg h(b)=\bot,

where we used (B) in the first step. Thus,
B
satisfies (2).

Our phrasings of (B) and (D) as (2) and (3) have the clear benefit of not using negation, and therefore being applicable to all (and not just Boolean) bounded distributive lattices, which are models of
positive
modal logic. As for the phrasing of transitivity as (4), we simply found the symmetry to the euclidean property appealing.

15
allows us, for example, to generalize a classic result of algebraic (Boolean) modal logic due to Halmos
[
Hal58
]
to the positive setting:
an operator
h\colon UA\rightarrow UA
is a
quantifier
if it satisfies the inequations from (1), (3) and (5):

Corollary 17

(Halmos)
.

An operator on a bounded distributive lattice algebra is a quantifier if and only if its dual relation is an equivalence relation.

5.
Monoids, Comonoids and Residuation Algebras

In this section we investigate
residuation algebras
, as introduced by Gehrke
[
Geh16b
]
, which are ordered structures with residual operators similar to language derivatives.
After recalling some foundations, we divide our study into two steps:
First, we start with the simpler case of
complete
ordered structures, for which we prove a duality between certain complete residuation algebras and ordered monoids.
This result will then serve as the foundation for both major applications in Sections
5.5
and
6
: the duality for complete structures in particular restricts to finite structures, since finite lattices are complete, which can then be extended to a duality for more general structures by forming appropriate completions.
On the other hand, the discrete duality for monoids is also the basis for the duality of the category of all categories.

5.1.
Foundations: Discrete Duality

We recall some facts and notation for distributive lattices and their complete counterparts. In particular, we describe the instantiation of the following discrete version of the extended duality setting from Diagram (
4.1
):
