\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(5.1)

(Complete) Semilattices

We denote the category of meet-semilattices with a top element, which is isomorphic to
\mathbf{JSL}
, by

\mathbf{MSL}
. It has a monoidal structure
given by tensor product
\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}
of meet-semilattices.
The category
\mathbf{MSL}
is dual to the category of Stone
meet-semilattices
[
HMS74
]
. Henceforth, we
denote the forgetful functors from
\mathbf{DL}
to
\mathbf{JSL}
and
\mathbf{MSL}
by

U_{\lor}
and
U_{\land}
, respectively, to avoid ambiguity.
The monad on
\mathbf{Priest}
induced by the dual of
F_{\land}\dashv U_{\land}

maps a Priestley space
X
to its hyperspace
{\mathbb{V}_{\uparrow}}X
of closed

upsets

[
BHM23
]
. The respective comonads on
\mathbf{DL}
for the adjunctions
F_{\land}\dashv U_{\land}
and

F_{\lor}\dashv U_{\lor}
are not isomorphic but

conjugate
:

F_{\land}U_{\land}\cong(F_{\lor}U_{\lor}(-)^{\partial})^{\partial}
, where
X^{\partial}
denotes the
order-dual
of
X
.
Their restrictions to the category of Boolean algebras are
isomorphic since their dual monads on
\mathbf{Stone}
satisfy

\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}=\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{V}_{\uparrow}
.

Similarly, the category
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}
(
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}
) consists of complete join- (meet-) semilattices with morphisms preserving all joins (meets).
Note that every complete join- or meet-semilattice
X
also has all meets and joins, respectively, given by

\bigwedge A=\bigvee\{x\mid\forall a\in A\colon x\leq a\},\qquad\bigvee A=\bigwedge\{x\mid\forall a\in A\colon x\geq a\}.

The categories
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}
and
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}
are easily seen to be dual to each other by swapping joins for meets and taking right, respectively left adjoints of morphisms. This duality is often stated equivalently as
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}
, if the duality also reverses the order on objects.
Analogously to its finitary counterpart,
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}
has a monoidal structure given by the complete tensor product
\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}
representing
\bigvee
-bilinear maps, making the left adjoints
\mathcal{D}\colon(\mathbf{Pos},\times)\rightarrow(\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee},\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}})
and
\mathcal{P}\colon(\mathbf{Set},\times)\rightarrow(\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee},\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}})
strong monoidal (the anlogous structure for
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}
is denoted
\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}
).
Analogously to the finitary setting, the tensor product of complete join-semilattices
J,J^{\prime}
is a quotient of
\mathcal{D}(|J|\times|J^{\prime}|)
, that is, it is presented by generators
(j,j^{\prime})\in|J|\times|J^{\prime}
, which we also write as
j\mathbin{{\otimes}}j^{\prime}
, modulo the equations

(\bigvee_{j\in A})\mathbin{{\otimes}}(\bigvee_{j^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}})=\bigvee_{j\in A,j^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}}j\mathbin{{\otimes}}j^{\prime}\qquad\text{ for all }A\subseteq J,A^{\prime}\subseteq J^{\prime}.

The monad on
\mathbf{Pos}
induced by this adjunction is the
downset monad

\mathcal{D}
. It
maps a set
X
to the set of all downward closed subsets of
X
. Its unit and multiplication are given by
{\downarrow}(-)\colon X\rightarrow\mathcal{D}X
and union, respectively, and its
\times
-monoidal structure simply takes products of downsets:

\hat{\delta}\colon\mathcal{D}X\times\mathcal{D}Y\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(X\times Y),\quad(A,B)\mapsto A\times B.

The Kleisli category of
\mathcal{D}
is the category
\mathbf{OrdRel}
of posets with
order
relations
as morphisms, that is, those relations
R\subseteq X\times Y
between posets satisfying
x^{\prime}\geq xRy\geq y^{\prime}\Longrightarrow x^{\prime}Ry^{\prime}
.
We denote the lifting of the cartesian structure of
\mathbf{Pos}
to
\mathbf{OrdRel}
by
\bar{\times}
:
on objects of
\mathbf{OrdRel}
we have
X\bar{\times}Y=X\times Y
, and the tensor
r\bar{\times}r^{\prime}
of order relations
r\colon X\rightarrow\mathcal{D}Y
and
r^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}Y^{\prime}
is defined as

\hat{\delta}\cdot(r\times r^{\prime})\colon X\times X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}Y\times\mathcal{D}Y^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Y\times Y^{\prime}),\quad(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto\{(y,y^{\prime})\mid y\in r(x),y^{\prime}\in r(y)\}.

The order-discrete restriction
\mathcal{P}\colon\mathbf{Set}\dashv\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu|-|
induces the classical product monoidal structure on the powerset monad, and its Kleisli category
\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{P}}
is well-known to be the category of sets and relations.

Completely Distributive Lattices.

We denote the completely join-prime elements of an ACDL
D
by
\mathcal{J}D\cong\mathbf{ACDL}(D,2)
and the atoms of a CABA
B
by
\mathcal{A}B\cong\mathbf{CABA}(B,2)
.
The left adjoint to the forgetful functor
V_{\land}\colon\mathbf{ACDL}\rightarrow\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}
takes downsets, i.e.
\mathcal{D}\dashv V_{\land}
, and its unit
M\rightarrow\mathcal{D}V_{\land}
is given by

\eta_{M}\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{D}M,\quad x\mapsto\{y\mid y\leq x\}.

Note that the left adjoint of the forgetful functor
\mathbf{CABA}\rightarrow\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}
is given by
\mathcal{P}
, with the same unit
\eta_{M}
.
If we respectively equip
\mathbf{ACDL}
and
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}
with coproduct
+
and tensor product
\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}
of complete meet-semilattices as monoidal structures, then the forgetful functor
(\mathbf{ACDL},+)\rightarrow(\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge},\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}})
is strong monoidal, analogously to the forgetful functor
(\mathbf{DL},+)\rightarrow(\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge},\mathbin{{\boxtimes}})
; the same holds for the forgetful functor
(\mathbf{CABA},+)\rightarrow(\mathbf{MSL},\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}})
.
A straightforward verification shows that the adjunctions
\mathcal{D}\dashv V_{\land}
and
\mathcal{D}\dashv|-|
are dual with respect to the dualities
\mathbf{ACDL}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{Pos}
and
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}
.
For detailed verifications on the order-discrete setting we refer the reader to Bezhanishvili et al.
[
BCM22
]
.

Remark 18
.

Restricting to finite carriers, the diagrams (
4.1
) and (
5.1
) coincide, since

\mathbf{ACDL}_{\mathrm{f}}=\mathbf{DL}_{\mathrm{f}},\qquad\mathbf{JSL}_{\mathrm{f}}\cong\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee,{\mathrm{f}}},\qquad\mathbf{Priest}_{\mathrm{f}}\cong\mathbf{Pos}_{\mathrm{f}},

and so the results we establish in the following for complete structures will in particular hold for finite ones.

{nota}

We tacitly omit the
forgetful functors
U_{\land}
and
U_{\lor}
for notational brevity, whenever they are
clear from the context, and just write the join- and meet-semilattice tensor products
of the underlying
semilattices of distributive lattices lattices
D,D^{\prime}
as
D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D^{\prime}
and
D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}D^{\prime}
, respectively.
The same holds for the complete versions: we omit the functors
V_{\land},V_{\lor}

and write
D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D^{\prime}
and
D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D^{\prime}
for the complete tensor products of ACDLs
D

and
D^{\prime}
.

Remark 19

(Adjunctions on Lattices)
.

It is well known that a
monotone map
f\colon D\rightarrow D^{\prime}
between complete
lattices preserves all joins if and only if it has a right adjoint

f_{*}\colon D^{\prime}\rightarrow D
, which is then given by

f_{*}(d^{\prime})=\V_{f(d)\leq d^{\prime}}d
; dually, it preserves all meets
iff it has a left adjoint
f^{*}\colon D^{\prime}\rightarrow D
, given
by
f^{*}(d^{\prime})=\A_{d^{\prime}\leq f(d)}d
. The
join-primes
\mathcal{J}D
of a bounded distributive lattice
D
are
precisely those elements
p\in D
whose characteristic function

\chi_{p}\colon D\rightarrow 2
(mapping
x\in D
to
1

iff
p\leq x
) is a homomorphism. The left adjoint of

\chi_{p}
, denoted
p\colon 2\rightarrow D
, is the join-semilattice morphism
defined by
1\mapsto p
.

Lemma 20
.

(1)

The join- and meet-semilattice tensor products of bounded distributive lattices
D,E
yield isomorphic lattices, i.e. there exists an isomorphism

\omega\colon U_{\lor}D\mathbin{{\otimes}}U_{\lor}E\xrightarrow{~\cong~}U_{\land}D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}U_{\land}E

satisfying
\omega(d\mathbin{{\otimes}}1)=d\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}0
and
\omega(1\mathbin{{\otimes}}e)=0\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}e
.

(2)

Adjunctions on bounded distributive lattices ‘compose horizontally’:
Given adjunctions
f\colon D\dashv E\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mug
and
f^{\prime}\colon D^{\prime}\dashv E^{\prime}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mug^{\prime}
between distributive lattices we get adjunctions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\otimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime}}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\otimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime}}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\otimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\mathbin{{\otimes}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}}

If the right adjoints
g
and
g^{\prime}
preserve finite joins, then this simplifies to

f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}\dashv g\mathbin{{\otimes}}g^{\prime}=\omega^{-1}(g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime})\omega.

Dually,
\omega(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})\omega^{-1}=f\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}f^{\prime}
, if
f
and
f^{\prime}
preserve finite meets.

Proof 5.1
.

(1)

We have already seen that for bounded distributive lattices
D,E
their
\mathbf{JSL}
tensor product
U_{\lor}D\mathbin{{\otimes}}U_{\lor}E
is their coproduct in
\mathbf{DL}
.
But by order-duality, the meet-semilattice tensor product
U_{\land}D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}U_{\land}E
also gives a representation of the coproduct
D+E
in
\mathbf{DL}
:
its inclusions
\hat{\iota}_{1},\hat{\iota}_{2}
map
d\in D,e\in E
to
\hat{\iota}_{1}(d)=d\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}0
and
\hat{\iota}_{2}(e)=0\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}e
, respectively.
By the universal property of the coproduct we obtain a unique isomorphism

\omega\colon U_{\lor}D\mathbin{{\otimes}}U_{\lor}E\rightarrow U_{\land}D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}U_{\land}E\qquad\text{such that}\qquad\text{$\omega\cdot\iota_{i}=\hat{\iota}_{i}$ for $i=1,2$.}

We now show that
\omega
is given by the following concrete formula:

\V_{i\in I}d_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}e_{i}\mapsto\A_{A\in\mathcal{P}I}\big(\V_{i\in A}d_{i}\big)\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\big(\V_{i\not\in A}e_{i}\big).

By definition, the canonical isomorphism
\omega
is the coparing
\omega=[\hat{\iota}_{1},\hat{\iota}_{2}]
of the inclusions of the meet-semilattice tensor product.
Therefore on pure tensors
\omega
maps
d\mathbin{{\otimes}}e\mapsto d\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}0\land 0\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}e
, which extends to general elements of
D\mathbin{{\otimes}}E
via distributivity as

\displaystyle\omega\big(\V_{i\in I}d_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}e_{i}\big)

\displaystyle=\V_{i\in I}\omega(d_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}e_{i})

\displaystyle=\V_{i\in I}d_{i}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}0\land 0\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\A_{A\in\mathcal{P}I}\V_{i\in A}d_{i}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}0\lor\V_{i\not\in A}0\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\A_{A\in\mathcal{P}I}\big(\V_{i\in A}d_{i}\big)\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}0\lor 0\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\big(\V_{i\not\in A}e_{i}\big)

\displaystyle=\A_{A\in\mathcal{P}I}\big(\V_{i\in A}d_{i}\big)\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\big(\V_{i\not\in A}e_{i}\big),

where we use in the last two steps that joins in
D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}E
satisfy the equation

(a\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}b)\lor(c\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}d)=(a\lor c)\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}(b\lor d).

Note that by order-duality the inverse
\omega^{-1}
is given by

\A_{i\in I}d_{i}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}e_{i}\mapsto\V_{A\in\mathcal{P}I}\big(\A_{i\in A}d_{i}\big)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\big(\A_{i\not\in A}e_{i}\big).

(2)

We only need to prove that one of the squares is an adjunction, since we obtain all others by suitable composition with
\omega
and its inverse. We show this for the third diagram, that is, we show that there is an adjunction

(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})\cdot\omega^{-1}\dashv(g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime})\cdot\omega

by verifying the unit and counit inequalities

\mathrm{id}\leq(g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime})\cdot\omega\cdot(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})\cdot\omega^{-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})\cdot\omega^{-1}\cdot(g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime})\cdot\omega\leq\mathrm{id}.

We only prove the counit inequality; the proof of the unit inequality is dual. Recall that the right adjoint
g
preserves meets.
Given a finite index set
A
we write
x_{A}=\V_{i\in A}x_{i}
and compute for every element
\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i}\in D\mathbin{{\otimes}}E

\displaystyle(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})\omega^{-1}(g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime})\omega(\V_{i}x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})\omega^{-1}(g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime})(\A_{A\in\mathcal{P}I}x_{A}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}y_{A^{c}})

\displaystyle\text{def.\ }\omega

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})\omega^{-1}(\A_{A\in\mathcal{P}I}g(x_{A})\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime}(y_{A^{c}}))

\displaystyle\text{def.\ }g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})(\V_{B\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}I}(\A_{A\in B}g(x_{A}))\mathbin{{\otimes}}(\A_{A\in B^{c}}g^{\prime}(y_{A^{c}})))

\displaystyle\text{def.\ }\omega^{-1}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime})(\V_{B\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}I}g(\A_{A\in B}x_{A})\mathbin{{\otimes}}g^{\prime}(\A_{A\in B^{c}}y_{A^{c}}))

\displaystyle g\text{ preserves meets}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(\V_{B\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}I}fg(\A_{A\in B}x_{A})\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}g^{\prime}(\A_{A\in B^{c}}y_{A^{c}}))

\displaystyle\text{def.\ }f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle(\V_{B\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}I}\mathrm{id}(\A_{A\in B}x_{A})\mathbin{{\otimes}}\mathrm{id}(\A_{A\in B^{c}}y_{A^{c}}))

\displaystyle\text{counits }f\dashv g,f^{\prime}\dashv g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}\omega(\V_{i}x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i})=\V_{i}x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i}.

As for the last statement, if
g
and
g^{\prime}
preserve finite joins, then
g\mathbin{{\otimes}}g^{\prime}
is defined (otherwise it would not be!), and it is clear that
f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{\prime}\dashv g\mathbin{{\otimes}}g^{\prime}
.
By uniqueness of adjoints this implies
g\mathbin{{\otimes}}g^{\prime}=\omega^{-1}(g\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}g^{\prime})\omega
.

Remark 21
.

20
holds analogously for complete tensor products of ACDLs: there exists a unique isomorphism
\omega\colon V_{\lor}D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}V_{\lor}E\simeq V_{\land}D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}V_{\land}E
commuting with the coproduct injections.

Proposition 22
.

Let
D
be an ACDL.

(1)

For every
x\in D
the
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}
-morphism

x\mathbin{{\otimes}}(-)\colon D\rightarrow D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D,\qquad y\mapsto x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y,

has a right adjoint

\displaystyle x\multimap(-)\colon D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D\quad

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad D

\displaystyle T\quad\quad

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad\V_{x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\leq T}y

called
tensor implication
.
It can be extended to a binary function

(-)\multimap(-)\colon D^{\partial}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}(D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D)\rightarrow D.

Analogously,
(-)\otimes x\colon D\rightarrow D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D
has a right adjoint
(-){\,{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}\,}x\colon D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D\to D
.

(2)

If
p\in\mathcal{J}D
is completely join-prime then
p\multimap(-)
is a lattice homomorphism given by

\lambda\cdot(\chi_{p}+\mathrm{id})\colon D+D\rightarrow 2+D\cong D,\qquad\V_{i\in I}p_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}q_{i}\mapsto\V_{p\leq p_{i}}q_{i}.

(3)

Every adjunction
l\colon E\dashv D\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mur
between ACDLs satisfies

x\multimap\omega^{-1}(r\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}r)\omega(T)=r(l(x)\multimap T)

as well as

l(x\multimap T)\leq l(x)\multimap(l\mathbin{{\otimes}}l)(T)\qquad{\text{and}}\qquad r(x\multimap T)\leq r(x)\multimap\omega^{-1}(r\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}r)\omega(T),

where the latter inequality holds with equality if
r
is order-reflecting.

Proof 5.2
.

(1)

The function
x\mathbin{{\otimes}}(-)
preserves joins by definition, so its right adjoint
x\multimap(-)
exists and is given by
T\mapsto\V_{x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\leq T}y
.
We can write
x\mathbin{{\otimes}}(-)
as

x\mathbin{{\otimes}}(-)=\lambda^{-1}\cdot(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}\mathrm{id})\colon D\cong 2\mathbin{{\otimes}}D\rightarrow D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D.

By
20
(2)
, this has the right adjoint
\lambda\cdot\omega^{-1}\cdot(\chi_{x}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})\cdot\omega
.
The extension to a binary function
(-)\multimap(-)\colon D^{\partial}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}(D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D)\rightarrow D
is an instance of an
adjunction with a parameter
(cf.
[
ML98
, Ch. IV.7]
).

(2)

If
x=p\in\mathcal{J}D
is join-prime, then
\chi_{p}
preserves joins. Hence,
p\multimap(-)
simplifies to

\lambda^{-1}\cdot(\chi_{p}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\mathrm{id})\colon D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D\rightarrow 2\mathbin{{\otimes}}D\cong 2,\quad\V_{i}p_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}q_{i}\mapsto\V_{p\leq p_{i}}q_{i},

which is an ACDL morphism since
\lambda^{-},\chi_{p}
and
\mathrm{id}
are.

(3)

Let
T\in D\mathbin{{\otimes}}D
and
x\in E
. Then for all
y\in E
we have

\displaystyle y\leq x\multimap\omega^{-1}(r\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}r)\omega(T)

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\leq\omega^{-1}(r\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}r)\omega(T)

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,l(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}l(y)\leq T

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,l(y)\leq l(x)\multimap T

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,y\leq r(l(x)\multimap T).

So the first statement follows. Using this we compute

x\multimap T\leq x\multimap\omega^{-1}(r\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}r)\omega(l\mathbin{{\otimes}}l)(T)=r(l(x)\multimap(l\mathbin{{\otimes}}l)(T)),

which by adjoint transposition is equivalent to

l(x\multimap T)\leq l(x)\multimap(l\mathbin{{\otimes}}l)(T).

Similarly,

r(x\multimap T)\leq r(l(r(x))\multimap T)=r(x)\multimap\omega^{-1}(r\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}r)\omega(T),

and the first step is an equality if
l\cdot r=\mathrm{id}
, which is equivalent to
r
being order-reflecting.

5.2.
Residuation Algebras

We proceed with recalling the definition of residuation algebras
[
Geh16b
]
, which are distributive lattices equipped with two additional operations to be thought of as abstractions of language derivatives (Example
5.2
.
(4)
).
We furthermore introduce an obvious extension of residuation algebras for the complete setting.

{defi}

(1)

A
(Boolean) residuation algebra
consists of a (Boolean)
lattice
R\in\mathbf{DL}
equipped with
\mathbf{MSL}
-morphisms

\setminus\colon R^{\partial}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}R\rightarrow R
and

\mathrel{/}\colon R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}R^{\partial}\rightarrow R
, the

left
and
right residual
, satisfying the

residuation property
:

b\leq a\setminus c\,\Longleftrightarrow\,a\leq c\mathrel{/}b
.

(2)

A
residuation ACDL
(
CABA
)
R
is an ACDL (CABA) whose residuals are complete morphisms
R^{\partial}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R\rightarrow R\leftarrow R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R^{\partial}
.

(3)

A residuation algebra
R
is
associative
if it satisfies

x\setminus(z\mathrel{/}y)=(x\setminus z)\mathrel{/}y\qquad\text{for all $x,y,z\in R$},

and it is
(prime-)unital

if there exists a (join-prime) element

e\in R
which is a
unit
:

e\setminus z=z=z\mathrel{/}e.

Remark 23
.

Units are unique: If

e,e^{\prime}
are units, then
e=e^{\prime}\setminus e
since
e^{\prime}
is a unit,
so by the residuation property
e^{\prime}\leq e\mathrel{/}e=e
.
Analogously we have
e\leq e^{\prime}
, so
e=e^{\prime}
.

As indicated above, residuals serve as algebraic generalizations of language
derivatives, but as the following examples indicate they are not limited to this
interpretation.

{exas}

(1)

Every Heyting algebra is
an associative residuation algebra with residuals

a\setminus c=a\rightarrow c
and
c\mathrel{/}b=b\rightarrow c
.

(2)

Every Boolean algebra
B
is a
non-associative residuation algebra with
x\setminus 1=1
and
x\setminus z=\neg x
for
z\neq 1
. If
B
is non-trivial, then it is not prime-unital by the first equality.

(3)

Every continuous binary function
f\colon X\times X\rightarrow X
on
a Stone space
X
induces a
residuation algebra on its dual Boolean algebra
\widehat{X}
of clopens: given
A,B,C\in\widehat{X}
, put

\displaystyle A\setminus C

\displaystyle=\{x\in X\mid\forall a\in A\colon f(a,x)\in C\},

\displaystyle C\mathrel{/}B

\displaystyle=\{x\in X\mid\forall b\in B\colon f(x,b)\in C\}.

(4)

The set
\reg\Sigma
of all regular
languages over a finite alphabet
\Sigma
forms an associative
Boolean residuation algebra with residuals given by left and right

extended derivatives
:

K\setminus L=\{v\in\Sigma^{*}\mid Kv\subseteq L\},\qquad L\mathrel{/}K=\{v\in\Sigma^{*}\mid vK\subseteq L\}.

The unit is the singleton
\{\varepsilon\}
, where
\varepsilon
is the empty word.
This example is a special case
of
(3)
: take as
(X,f)

the
free profinite monoid
on
\Sigma
, which is the Stone dual of
\reg\Sigma
.

We now introduce the notion of a
residuation
morphism
between residuation algebras and also its
relational
generalization.

{defi}

(1)

A lattice morphism
f\colon R\rightarrow S
between prime-unital
residuation algebras is a
(pure) residuation morphism
if it
satisfies the conditions

\displaystyle\forall x,z\in R\colon f(x\setminus z)\leq f(x)\setminus f(z)\text{ and }f(z\mathrel{/}x)\leq f(z)\mathrel{/}f(x)

(Forth)

\displaystyle\forall(y,z)\in S\times R\colon\exists x_{y,z}\in R\colon y\leq f(x_{y,z})\text{ and }y\setminus f(z)=f(x_{y,z}\setminus z)

(Back)

\displaystyle\forall(y,z)\in S\times R\colon\exists x_{y,z}\in R\colon y\leq f(x_{y,z})\text{ and }f(z)\mathrel{/}y=f(z\mathrel{/}x_{y,z})

(Back’)

\displaystyle\forall x\in R\colon e\leq x\Leftrightarrow e^{\prime}\leq f(x)

(Unit)

where
e
and
e^{\prime}
are the units of
R
and
S
. The morphism
f
is
open
if, additionally, it has a left adjoint.
Prime-unital residuation algebras and residuation morphisms
form a category
\mathbf{Res}
.

(2)

A
corelational residuation morphism
from a
prime-unital residuation algebra
R
to a prime-unital residuation algebra
S
is a
morphism
\rho\in\mathbf{JSL}_{1}(R,S)
satisfying

\rho(x\setminus z)\leq\rho(x)\setminus\rho(z)\quad{\text{and}}\quad e^{\prime}\leq\rho(e).

Prime-unital residuation algebras with relational
morphisms form a category
\mathbf{RelRes}
.

{nota}

(1)

We use the convention that for a subcategory
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{Res}
or
\mathbf{RelRes}
we denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{C}
with Boolean carriers by
\mathbf{BC}
.

(2)

All categories above have obvious counterparts for residuation ACDLs and residuation CABAs with complete morphisms, which we denote by
\mathbf{ResACDL},\mathbf{RelResACDL}
, etc.

Remark 24
.

Let us provide some intuition behind
Section 5.2
.

(1)

The notion of residuation morphism is derived from a result by Gehrke

[
Geh16b
, Thm. 3.19]
, where it is shown to capture precisely
the conditions satisfied by the duals of morphisms of binary Stone
algebras.

(2)

We speak about
corelational
morphisms of residuation algebras
since for these will dualize precisely to relational
morphisms of monoids. Recall that a
relational morphism

a monoid
M
to a monoid
N
is a total relation

\rho\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{+}N
satisfying

\rho(x)\rho(y)\subseteq\rho(xy)\qquad\text{and}\qquad 1_{N}\in\rho(1_{M}).

(5.2)

Relational morphisms represent inverses of surjective monoid
homomorphisms
[
RS09
, p. 38]
. More precisely, the inverse relation
h^{-1}
of a surjective monoid
homomorphism
h\colon N\twoheadrightarrow M
is a relational morphism; conversely, if a relational morphism

h^{-1}\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{+}N
is the inverse of a function

h\colon N\rightarrow M
, then
h
is a
surjective monoid homomorphism.

Categorically, we can consider an
inverse relation
e^{-1}\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}N
of a surjective map

e\colon N\twoheadrightarrow M
as is its
right adjoint
in the
order-enriched category
\mathbf{Rel}\simeq\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{P}}
of sets and
relations: as relations they satisfy
\mathrm{id}_{N}\leq e^{-1}\cdot e
and

e\cdot e^{-1}\leq\mathrm{id}_{M}
. Under duality the composition is reversed,
so an inverse relation
e^{-1}
dualizes to a
left adjoint

\widehat{e^{-1}}\dashv\widehat{e}
. Since left adjoints between
lattices are precisely the join-preserving functions, this justifies
our choice that corelational morphisms of residuation algebras preserve
(finite) joins (and not necessarily meets). Note also that totality of

e^{-1}
is equivalent to surjectivity of
e
, which by

14
dualizes to the property that

\widehat{e^{-1}}
preserves the top element.

(3)

This is also the rationale behind the naming for
open

residuation morphisms: if
e\colon M\twoheadrightarrow N
is a continuous
surjection between Stone monoids then
e^{-1}\colon N\rightarrow\mathbb{V}M
is
continuous precisely iff
e
is an open map.

For open residuation morphisms the three conditions (
Back
),
(
Forth
), (
Unit
) can be replaced by two equivalent, yet much
simpler conditions. Over complete residuation algebras this is particularly
convenient, since every residuation morphism is open.

Lemma 25
.

Let
R,S
be prime-unital residuation algebras. A lattice
morphism
f\colon R\rightarrow S
with a left adjoint
f^{*}\colon S\rightarrow R
is an open residuation
morphism iff it satisfies the equations

f^{*}(e^{\prime})=e,\qquad\qquad\forall y\in S,z\in R\colon y\setminus f(z)=f(f^{*}(y)\setminus z),

(Open)

and the same equation for the right residual
\mathrel{/}
.

In the proof below we omit mentioning the right residual because the
arguments for it are completely analogous. This will be the case in
most of the subsequent proofs involving properties of residuals.

Proof 5.3
.

We first show that if
f
satisfies (
Open
), then it is an open residuation morphism.
The (
Forth
) condition follows from
f^{*}\cdot f\leq\mathrm{id}
and contravariance of
\setminus
in the first argument:

f(x\setminus z)\leq f(f^{*}(f(x))\setminus z)=f(x)\setminus f(z).

For the (
Back
) condition, given
y\in S
and
z\in R
we choose the element
x_{y,z}=f^{*}(y)\in R
(independently of
z
).
The unit of the adjunction yields
y\leq f(f^{*}(y))=f(x_{y,z})
, and using (
Open
) we obtain

y\setminus f(z)=f(f^{*}(y)\setminus z)=f(x_{y,z}\setminus z).

For the other direction, we prove that every open residuation morphism satisfies the condition (
Open
).
Let
(y,z)\in S\times R
. By the (
Back
) condition, there exists
x_{y,z}\in R
such that
y\leq f(x_{y,z})
and
y\setminus f(z)=f(x_{y,z}\setminus z)
.
This implies
f^{*}(y)\leq x_{y,z}
, and using (
Back
) and contravariance of
\setminus
in the first argument, we obtain

y\setminus f(z)=f(x_{y,z}\setminus z)\leq f(f^{*}(y)\setminus z).

On the other hand, the adjunction unit
y\leq f(f^{*}(y))
, (
Forth
) and contravariance of
\setminus
yield

f(f^{*}(y)\setminus z)\leq f(f^{*}(y))\setminus f(z)\leq y\setminus f(z).

This proves that
f
indeed satisfies (
Open
).

For the respective unitality conditions we have by
f^{*}\dashv f
that

\forall x\colon e\leq x\Leftrightarrow e^{\prime}\leq f(x)\Leftrightarrow f^{*}(e^{\prime})\leq x,

which is equivalent to
e=f^{*}(e^{\prime})
.

{exa}

Let
\Sigma
and
\Delta
be finite alphabets. Every substitution

f_{0}\colon\Sigma\rightarrow\Delta^{*}
can be extended to a
monoid homomorphism
f\colon\Sigma^{*}\rightarrow\Delta^{*}
,
and for regular languages
L\in\reg\Sigma
and
K\in\reg\Delta
, both
f[L]
and
f^{-1}[K]
are also regular.
Then

f^{-1}\colon\reg\Delta\rightarrow\reg\Sigma
is an open
residuation morphism. Indeed, its left adjoint is given by the direct image map
f[-]\colon\reg\Sigma\rightarrow\reg\Delta
, satisfying
f[\{\varepsilon\}]=\{f(\varepsilon)\}=\{\varepsilon\}
and

K\setminus f^{-1}[L]=\{w\mid Kw\subseteq f^{-1}[L]\}=\{w\mid f[K]f(w)\subseteq L\}=f^{-1}(f[K]\setminus L).

5.3.
Residuation ACDLs

We start by investigating complete residuation algebras, whose characterization
(
32
) in terms of coalgebras forms not only the backbone of the
classification and duality theory of
locally finite
residuation algebras in

Section 5.4
and
5.5
,

but also of the duality for the category of small categories in
Section 6
.
Concretely, we use the tensor implication operator introduced in the last section to associate a
comultiplication
to the residuals and investigate its properties.

Construction 26
.

(1)

Every
V_{\lor}
-algebra

V_{\lor}D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}V_{\lor}D\rightarrow V_{\lor}D

on an
ACDL
D
has a right adjoint

\gamma\colon V_{\land}D\rightarrow V_{\land}(D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D)
that can be extended, by
using the isomorphism
\omega
from
20
,
to a
V_{\land}
-coalgebra

\bar{\gamma}=V_{\land}\omega\cdot\gamma\colon V_{\land}D\rightarrow V_{\land}(D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D)\cong V_{\land}(D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D)=V_{\land}D\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}V_{\land}D.

We refer to both versions
\gamma
and
\bar{\gamma}
as
comultiplication
or
coalgebra structure
.
Conversely, we obtain a
V_{\lor}
-algebra from a comultiplication
\gamma\colon V_{\land}D\rightarrow V_{\land}(D\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}D)
by taking its left adjoint.

(2)

In a residuation ACDL
R
the partially applied
residuals have respective left adjoints

\mu(x,-)\dashv(x\setminus-)
and
\mu(-,y)\dashv(-\mathrel{/}y)
that
can be combined
into a
V_{\lor}
-algebra structure

\mu\colon V_{\lor}R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}V_{\lor}R\rightarrow V_{\lor}R

that we call
multiplication
. By part
(1)
, the multiplication
\mu
induces the comultiplication
\gamma\colon V_{\land}R\rightarrow V_{\land}(R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R)
, or
\bar{\gamma}\colon V_{\land}R\rightarrow V_{\land}R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}V_{\land}R
.

Each of the operators
\mathrel{/}
,
\setminus
,
\mu
,
\gamma
determines the others uniquely due to the equivalences

x\leq z\mathrel{/}y\iff y\leq x\setminus z\iff\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)\leq z\iff x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\leq\gamma(z).

The following lemma provides the concrete formulas.

Lemma 27
.

Let
R
be a residuation ACDL.

(1)

The residuals can be calculated from the comultiplication:

x\setminus z=x\multimap\gamma(z)\quad\text{ and }\quad z\mathrel{/}y=\gamma(z){\,{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}\,}y,

where
\multimap
and

\multimap

are the tensor implications given by
22
.

(2)

The comultiplication can be calculated from the residuals:

\gamma(z)=\V_{x\in R}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}(x\setminus z)=\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}R}p\mathbin{{\otimes}}(p\setminus z).

Proof 5.4
.

(1)

For all
x,y,z\in R
we have

y\leq x\setminus z\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)\leq z\,\Longleftrightarrow\,x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\leq\gamma(z)\,\Longleftrightarrow\,y\leq x\multimap\gamma(z),

and analogously
x\leq z\mathrel{/}y=\gamma(z){\,{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}\,}y
.

(2)

We compute

\displaystyle\gamma(z)

\displaystyle=\V\{\V_{i}x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i}\mid x_{i},y_{i}\in R,\mu(\V_{i}x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i})\leq z\}

formula for right adjoint

\displaystyle=\V\{x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\mid x,y\in R,\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)\leq z\}

\mu
preserves joins

\displaystyle=\V\{x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\mid x,y\in R,y\leq x\setminus z\}

\displaystyle\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}-)\dashv(x\setminus-)

\displaystyle=\V_{x\in R}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z

simplification.

It is clear that
\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}R}p\mathbin{{\otimes}}p\setminus z\leq\V_{x\in R}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z
,
for the reverse inclusion we compute

x\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z=(\V_{p\leq x}p)\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z=\V_{p\leq x}p\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z\leq\V_{p\leq x}p\mathbin{{\otimes}}p\setminus z,

where
p
ranges over
\mathcal{J}R
and we use contravariance of
(-\setminus z)
in the last step.

We now investigate when the comultiplication is
pure
,
that is, lifts to a complete lattice morphism
R\rightarrow R+R
and thus corresponds to a pure multiplication.

Lemma 28
.

For a residuation ACDL
R
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

The comultiplication is pure: for all
A\subseteq R
we have

\gamma(\bigvee_{x\in A}x)=\bigvee_{a\in A}\gamma(x)
.

(2)

For all
p\in\mathcal{J}R
and
A\subseteq R

we have

p\setminus(\bigvee_{x\in A}x)=\bigvee_{x\in A}p\setminus x\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\bigvee_{x\in A})\mathrel{/}p=\bigvee_{x\in A}x\mathrel{/}p

(3)

For all
x,y\in R\colon\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)=0\iff x=0\lor y=0
,
and
\mu[\mathcal{J}(R+R)]\subseteq\mathcal{J}R
.

Proof 5.5
.

(1)

\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

(3)
. First, we have

\displaystyle\gamma(0)=0

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\gamma(0)\leq 0

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\forall T\colon T\leq\gamma(0)\Rightarrow T\leq 0

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\forall T=\V_{i}x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i}\colon\mu(T)=\V_{i}\mu(x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i})\leq 0\Rightarrow\forall i\colon x_{i}\mathbin{{\otimes}}y_{i}\leq 0

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\forall x,y\colon\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)=0\Rightarrow x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y=0

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\forall x,y\colon\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)=0\Leftrightarrow x=0\lor y=0,

where we use in the penultimate equivalence that
\mu(0)=0
, and in the last equivalence that
x\otimes y=0
iff
x=0
or
y=0
.
To show that
\gamma
preserves joins, note that the join-primes of
R\mathbin{{\otimes}}R
are given by pure tensors of
p\mathbin{{\otimes}}q
of join-primes
p,q\in\mathcal{J}R
and that in a distributive lattice an element
j
is join-prime iff it is is
join-prime
: for
A\subseteq R
if
j\leq\bigvee A
then
j\leq x
for some
x\in R
.
Given
A\subseteq R
, we compute:

\displaystyle\gamma(\bigvee_{x\in A}x)=\bigvee_{x\in A}\gamma(x)

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{J}R:a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b\leq\bigvee_{x\in A}\gamma(x)\Rightarrow a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b\leq\bigvee_{x\in A}\gamma(x)

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{J}R:a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b\leq\gamma(\bigvee_{x\in A}x)\Rightarrow[\exists x\in A\colon a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b\leq\gamma(x)]

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{J}R:\mu(a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b)\leq\bigvee_{x\in A}x\Rightarrow[\exists x\in A\colon\mu(a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b)\leq x]

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b\in\mathcal{J}[R\mathbin{{\otimes}}R]:\mu(a\mathbin{{\otimes}}b)\in\mathcal{J}R.

For the equivalence
(1)

\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

(2)
, we combine
27
with the preservation properties of
x\multimap(-)
from
22
(1)
:
If
\gamma
is pure, then it preserves joins and so does
(p\setminus-)=p\multimap\gamma(-)
for
p\in\mathcal{J}R
;
and if every
(p\setminus-)
preserves joins, then so does
\gamma=\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}R}p\mathbin{{\otimes}}p\setminus(-)
.

Next we show how structural identities like (co-)associativity or
unitality translate between
\gamma
,
\mu
and the residuals. Note that while the
statements are to be expected, the proof is non-trivial due to the
complication introduced by the seemingly innocent isomorphism

\omega\colon R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R\cong R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R
. Recall that a coalgebra

c\colon R\to R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R
is

coassociative
if
(c\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})\cdot c=(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}c)\cdot c
and
(prime-)counital
if it is equipped with a (prime)
counit

\varepsilon\in\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}(R,2)
(
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{ACDL}(R,2)
) satisfying
(\varepsilon\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})\cdot c=\mathrm{id}=(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\varepsilon)\cdot c
.
Diagrammatically, these equations are dual to the well-known monoid equations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{id}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Lemma 29
.

The following are equivalent for a residuation ACDL
R
:

(1)

The comultiplication on
R
is
coassociative and has a (prime) counit
\varepsilon
.

(2)

The residuals are associative and

R
has a (prime) unit
e\in R
.

(3)

The multiplication
\mu
is
associative and has a (prime) unit, that is, there exists

e\in R
satisfying

\mu(e\mathbin{{\otimes}}-)=\mathrm{id}=\mu(-\mathbin{{\otimes}}e)
.

Proof 5.6
.

For the proof we only use ‘adjunctional calculus’. The equivalence
(3)

\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

(2)
follows from uniqueness of adjoints; to see this, we write associativity of
\mu
as

\forall x,y\colon\mu(-\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)\cdot\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}-)=\mu(x\mathbin{{\otimes}}-)\cdot\mu(-\mathbin{{\otimes}}y)

and associativity of the residuals as

\forall x,y\colon(x\setminus-)\cdot(-\mathrel{/}y)=(-\mathrel{/}y)\cdot(x\setminus-).

Since the respective left and right sides of the above equalities are adjoint, and adjoints
are unique, it is clear that one of the equations holds iff the other one does. The unit of
the residuals is the left adjoint of the comultiplication
\gamma
, which implies the
equivalence of the (co-)unit properties.

The equivalence
(1)

\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

(3)
is shown similarly, but we have to be careful, since
\mu
and
\bar{\gamma}
are adjoint only up to composition with the isomorphism
\omega\colon R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R\rightarrow R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R
.
By
20
(2)
, we have the following diagram of adjunctions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}(R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R)\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mu}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{id}}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\bar{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{id}}

The left and right diamonds come from the horizontal composition of adjunctions under the respective tensor products.
The bottom diamond is easily seen to commute.
If
\mu
is associative the top inner diamond commutes, and so by uniqueness the outer big diamond commutes by uniqueness of adjoints, proving
\bar{\gamma}
coassociative.
Dually, if
\bar{\gamma}
is coassociative, then
\mu
is associative.
The unit of
\mu
is the left adjoint of the counit of
\gamma
, so one is prime iff the other one is.

These lemmas suggest the following definitions:

{defi}

(1)

A residuation ACDL
R
is
pure
if it satisfies one of the equivalent conditions of
28
.

(2)

A

derivation ACDL
is a pure residuation ACDL that satisfies the equivalent conditions of
29
. We denote the respective full subcategories by

\mathbf{DerACDL}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{ResACDL}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{RelDerACDL}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{RelResACDL}.

(3)

A
V_{\land}
-coalgebra

\bar{\gamma}\colon V_{\land}C\rightarrow V_{\land}C\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}V_{\land}C
is a
V_{\land}
-comonoid
if it is
coassociative and prime-counital, and a

comonoid
if
\bar{\gamma}
is pure. We analogously define
U_{\land}
-comonoids
and (pure)
comonoids
in
\mathbf{DL}
.

In order to extend the correspondence of residuation ACDLs and
coalgebras to a categorical equivalence we introduce appropriate morphisms for coalgebras, which we also define for the general case of
U_{\land}
-coalgebras.

{defi}

(1)

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\land}C}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}

,

Comonoids with corelational morphisms form a category

.

(3)

Analogously, we define the category

and

Recall from
25
that every morphism of residuation ACDLs is open.

Proposition 30
.

Let
R
and
R^{\prime}
be prime-unital residuation ACDLs.

(1)

A morphism
f\in\mathbf{ACDL}(R,R^{\prime})
is a pure coalgebra morphism iff it is a residuation morphism.

(2)

If
R
and
R^{\prime}
are comonoids, then a morphism
\rho\in\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}(R,R^{\prime})
preserving the top element is a corelational comonoid morphism iff it is a corelational residuation morphism.

Proof 5.7
.

(1)

First, let
f
be a pure coalgebra morphism.
Then

\displaystyle x\setminus f(z)

\displaystyle=x\multimap\gamma^{\prime}f(z)

\displaystyle=x\multimap\omega^{-1}\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}f(z)

\displaystyle\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}=\omega\cdot\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=x\multimap\omega^{-1}(f\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\omega\gamma(z)

\displaystyle f\text{ is a coalgebra morphism}

\displaystyle=f(f^{*}(x)\multimap\gamma(z))

\displaystyle=f(f^{*}(x)\setminus z)

\displaystyle\text{\autoref{lem:gamma-res}},

which by
25
shows that
f
is
a residuation morphism.

Conversely, if
f
is a residuation morphism, then for every
z\in R
we compute

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}f(z)

\displaystyle=\V_{x^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}x^{\prime}\mathbin{{\otimes}}x^{\prime}\setminus f(z)

\displaystyle=\V_{x^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}x^{\prime}\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(f^{*}(x^{\prime})\setminus z)

f
residuation morphism

\displaystyle\leq\V_{x^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}ff^{*}(x^{\prime})\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(f^{*}(x^{\prime})\setminus z)

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}\leq f\cdot f^{*}

\displaystyle=(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)(\V_{x^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}f^{*}(x^{\prime})\mathbin{{\otimes}}f^{*}(x^{\prime})\setminus z)

\displaystyle f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f\text{ preserves joins}

\displaystyle\leq(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)(\V_{x\in R}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z)

\displaystyle f^{*}[R^{\prime}]\subseteq R

\displaystyle=(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\gamma(z)

\displaystyle\text{\autoref{lem:gamma-res}\ref{lem:gamma-res:2}}.

Now (order-isomorphic) postcomposition with
\omega
gives

\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}f=\omega\gamma^{\prime}f\leq\omega(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\gamma=(f\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\omega\gamma=(f\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\bar{\gamma}.

Conversely,

\displaystyle(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\gamma(z)

\displaystyle=\V_{x\in R}f(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(x\setminus z)

\displaystyle\leq\V_{x\in R}f(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(f^{*}f(x)\setminus z)

f^{*}f\leq\mathrm{id}
and contravariance

\displaystyle=\V_{x\in R}f(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(x)\setminus f(z)

f
is open and
25

\displaystyle\leq\V_{x^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}x^{\prime}\mathbin{{\otimes}}x^{\prime}\setminus f(z)

f[R]\subseteq R^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}(f(z))

\displaystyle\text{\autoref{lem:gamma-res}\ref{lem:gamma-res:2}}.

Postcomposition with
\omega
again yields
\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}f\geq(f\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\bar{\gamma}
.
Hence, we obtain
\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}f=(f\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}f)\bar{\gamma}
, so
f
is a pure coalgebra morphism. Moreover,
it is clear that the counit condition from
Section 5.3
(1)
is equivalent to the unit conditions from (
Open
), since (1)
e\dashv\epsilon
and
f^{*}(e)\dashv\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot f
, and (2) adjoints are unique, so either equations holds iff the other one does.

(2)

If
\rho\colon R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
is a corelational morphism of pure coalgebras, then

\displaystyle\rho(x\setminus z)

\displaystyle=\rho(x\multimap\gamma(z))

\displaystyle\leq\rho(x)\multimap(\rho\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\rho)(\gamma(z))

\displaystyle\leq\rho(x)\multimap\gamma^{\prime}(\rho(z))

\rho
corelational morphism

\displaystyle=\rho(x)\setminus\rho(z)

\displaystyle\text{\autoref{lem:gamma-res}\ref{lem:gamma-res:1}}.

Conversely, if
\rho\colon R\to R^{\prime}
is a corelational morphism of residuation algebras, then using
27
(2)
twice, and that
\rho[R]\subseteq R^{\prime}
, we obtain

(\rho\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\rho)\gamma(z)=\V_{x\in R}\rho(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\rho(x\setminus z)\leq\V_{x\in R}\rho(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\rho(x)\setminus\rho(z)\leq\gamma^{\prime}(\rho(z)).

For the respective counits we identify the neutral element
e\in R
with the
\mathbf{JSL}
-morphism
e\colon 2\rightarrow R
to compute

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\rho

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall x\colon\epsilon(x)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(\rho(x))

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall x,y\colon y\leq\epsilon(x)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow y\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(\rho(x))

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall x,y\colon e(y)\leq x

\displaystyle\Rightarrow e^{\prime}(y)\leq\rho(x)

\displaystyle e\dashv\epsilon,e^{\prime}\dashv\epsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\forall x\colon e\leq x

\displaystyle\Rightarrow e^{\prime}\leq\rho(x)

\displaystyle y\in\{0,1\}\text{ and }e(0)=e^{\prime}(0)=0

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle e^{\prime}\leq\rho(e),

where in the last step we set
x=e
for the downward implication, and the upward direction is simply monotonicity of
\rho
.

Theorem 31
.

The following categories are isomorphic

\coalg(V_{\land})\cong\mathbf{ResACDL},\quad\mathbf{Comon}\cong\mathbf{DerACDL},\quad\mathbf{RelComon}\cong\mathbf{RelDerACDL}.

Proof 5.8
.

The three isomorphisms are given on objects
by swapping between residuals and
comultiplication (note that the residual unit is a left adjoint of
the counit of the comultiplication), and act as identity on morphisms.

28
and
29

and
30
show that they are well-defined.

From
31
we obtain a dual characterization of ordered monoids; it restricts to a duality between ordinary monoids and derivation CABAs.
Recall that a
relational
morphism
of ordered monoids
M
and
N

is a total order-relation
\rho\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{+}N
(where
\mathcal{D}
is the downset monad) making the
following diagrams commute laxly:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}N\times\mathcal{D}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(N\times N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\times\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\delta}}

\leq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(\cdot_{N})}

\leq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

(5.3)

Theorem 32
.

(1)

The category of ordered monoids is dually equivalent to the
category of derivation ACDLs (or
ACDL-comonoids):

\mathbf{OrdMon}\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\mathbf{Comon}\;\cong\;\mathbf{DerACDL}.

(2)

The category of ordered monoids and relational morphisms is
dually equivalent to the category of derivation
ACDLs (or comonoids) and corelational
morphisms:

\mathbf{RelOrdMon}\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\mathbf{RelComon}\;\cong\;\mathbf{RelDerACDL}.

Proof 5.9
.

Both statements follow by extending the equivalences from
31

with the extended duality applied to the setting
Equation 5.1
established in
Section 5.1
: For
item (1)
we get that ordered monoids
M\times M\rightarrow M
are dual to comonoids
C\rightarrow C+C
in
\mathbf{ACDL}
, and for
item (2)
, this means that relational monoid morphisms
M\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{+}N
as in
5.3
dualize precisely to corelational morphisms
V_{\lor}N\rightarrow V_{\lor}M
of comonoids.

Remark 33
.

Both
31
and
32
restrict to finite carriers.
All finite lattices are complete and
\mathbf{Res}_{\mathrm{f}}=\mathbf{ResACDL}_{\mathrm{f}}
, so, writing
\mathbf{Der}_{\mathrm{f}}
for
\mathbf{DerACDL}_{\mathrm{f}}
, we get the equivalences

\mathbf{OrdMon}_{\mathrm{f}}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{f}}\cong\mathbf{Der}_{\mathrm{f}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{RelOrdMon}_{\mathrm{f}}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{RelComon}_{\mathrm{f}}\cong\mathbf{RelDer}_{\mathrm{f}}.

5.4.
Locally Finite Residuation Algebras

We now extend the correspondence between residuation algebras and coalgebras from
Section 5.3
from complete to non-complete carriers.
The main challenge arises from the reliance on adjoints for the constructions in
Section 5.3
, whose existence is of course not ensured for arbitrary distributive lattices as carriers.
We tackle this problem by considering
locally finite
structures, allowing us to extend the comultiplication
26
from finite subalgebras to the whole lattice.

We start with the motivating example from automata theory:
the residuals of regular languages from Example
5.2
.
(4)
.

{exa}

It is well known that the Boolean algebra
\reg\Sigma
of regular
languages dualizes under Stone duality to the Stone space
\overline{\Sigma^{*}}
of
profinite words
1
1

1

This space is commonly denoted
\widehat{\Sigma^{*}}
in the literature; we use the notation
\overline{\Sigma^{*}}
to avoid a clash with notation
\widehat{(-)}
for the dual equivalence.
(see e.g. Pippenger
[
Pip97
]
). The space
\overline{\Sigma^{*}}
can be constructed as the limit in the category of Stone spaces of the diagram of all finite quotient monoids of
\Sigma^{*}
, regarded as discrete spaces. It is equipped with a continuous monoid structure

\mu\colon\overline{{\Sigma}^{*}}\times\overline{{\Sigma}^{*}}\rightarrow\overline{{\Sigma}^{*}}

extending the concatenation of words.
We calculate below that its dual comultiplication
on regular languages under Stone duality is given as follows:

\displaystyle\gamma\colon\reg\Sigma

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad\reg\Sigma+\reg\Sigma

(5.4)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad\V_{[v]\in\mathrm{Syn}_{L}}[v]\mathbin{{\otimes}}[v]\setminus L,

where
\mathrm{Syn}_{L}
is the
syntactic monoid
of

L
; its elements are the equivalence classes of the
syntactic congruence relation
relation on
\Sigma^{*}
defined by

[v]=[w]\qquad\text{iff}\qquad\forall K,K^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\colon v\in K\setminus L\mathrel{/}K^{\prime}\iff w\in K\setminus L\mathrel{/}K^{\prime}.

The Stone monoid
\overline{\Sigma^{*}}
is
profinite
, that is, it is the (cofiltered) limit of the diagram of its finite continuous monoid quotients.
Therefore, by duality,
\reg\Sigma
is the filtered colimit of its finite sub-coalgebras
\mathcal{P}M\cong\widehat{M}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\overline{\Sigma^{*}}}\cong\reg\Sigma
dual to finite monoid quotients
\overline{\Sigma^{*}}\twoheadrightarrow M
.
This means that, given a regular language
L
, we can compute the
value of
\gamma(L)
using
\mathrm{Syn}(L)
as in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\reg\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cl(\overline{\Sigma^{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cl(\overline{\Sigma^{*}}\times\overline{\Sigma^{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cl(\overline{\Sigma^{*}})\!+\!\cl(\overline{\Sigma^{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\reg\Sigma\!+\!\reg\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{Syn}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Syn}_{L}\times\mathrm{Syn}_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{Syn}_{L}\!+\!\mathcal{P}\mathrm{Syn}_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h\times h)^{-1}}

Step

uses that

is finite; at step
