is regular.

The key to extending the duality of
33
is the notion of
residuation ideal
of a residuation algebra. It was introduced by Gehrke
[
Geh16b
]
to
characterize quotients of Priestley topological algebras.
In particular, she has shown
[
Geh16a
, Thm. 15]
that
the Stone monoid quotient
\mathrm{Syn}_{L}
of
\overline{{\Sigma}^{*}}
dualizes to the residuation ideal
generated by
L\in\reg\Sigma

{defi}

A
residuation ideal
of a residuation algebra
R
is a
sublattice
I\hookrightarrow R
closed under derivatives w.r.t. arbitrary
elements of
R
:

\forall z\in R,x\in I\colon x\setminus z\in I\text{ and }z\mathrel{/}x\in I.

In particular, every residuation ideal is a residuation subalgebra
of
R
.
We denote the residuation ideal generated by a subset
X\subseteq R
by
\setminus{X}/
.

Section 5.4
suggests a path to extending our constructions of
Section 5.3
from complete to more general residuation algebras: extend them
locally
, that is, by considering suitable finite substructures.
Note that in
the formula (
5.4
) for the comultiplication on regular languages it is
crucial that the residuation ideal
\setminus{\{L\}}/
generated
by a single regular language
L
is
finite
,
so that the join
(\#)
is defined.

{defi}

(1)

A residuation algebra
R
is
locally finite
if every
finite subset of
R
is contained in a finite residuation ideal
of
R
.

(2)

A
U_{\land}
-coalgebra
C
is
locally finite
if
every finite subset of
C
is contained in a finite subcoalgebra
of
C
.
The category of locally finite comonoids is denoted
\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{lf}}
.

Note that not every residuation algebra is locally finite, consider
for example an infinite Boolean algebra in Example
5.2
.
(2)
.

Proposition 34
.

(1)

Every locally finite residuation algebra
R
induces a locally
finite
U_{\land}
-coalgebra

\gamma_{\setminus}\colon U_{\land}R\rightarrow U_{\land}(R\mathbin{{\otimes}}R)
where

\gamma_{\setminus}(z)=(\iota_{A}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{A})(\gamma_{A}(z))=\V_{x\in A}\iota_{A}(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{A}(x\setminus z)=\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}A}\iota_{A}(p)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{A}(p\setminus z),

for every finite residuation ideal
\iota_{A}\colon A\hookrightarrow R

containing
z
(here
\gamma_{A}
is the comultiplication on
A
as in
26
).

(2)

Every locally finite
U_{\land}
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)

induces a locally finite residuation algebra where

x\setminus_{\gamma}z\;=\;\iota_{A}(x\multimap\gamma(z)),\qquad z\mathrel{/}_{\gamma}x\;=\;\iota_{A}(\gamma(z){\,{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}\,}x),

for every finite subcoalgebra

\iota_{A}\colon A\hookrightarrow C
containing
x,z
(here

\setminus_{A}
is the residual on
A
as given by

26
). The residuals have a canonical
presentation as

x\setminus_{\gamma}z\;=\;\iota_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(x)\setminus z)\qquad\text{and}\qquad z\mathrel{/}_{\gamma}x\;=\;\iota_{z}(z\mathrel{/}\iota_{z}^{*}(x))

where
\iota_{z}\colon\langle z\rangle\rightarrow C
is the
smallest (finite) subcoalgebra containing
z
.

(3)

These constructions are mutually inverse:

\gamma_{\setminus_{\gamma}}\;=\;\gamma\qquad\text{and}\qquad\setminus_{\gamma_{\setminus}}\;=\;\setminus.

Proof 5.10
.

(1a)

We first show that the comultiplication

\gamma_{\setminus}=(\iota_{A}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{A})(\gamma(z))
is well-defined, that is, it
does not depend on the residuation ideal
A
containing
z
. We
first prove an auxiliary statement:

Lemma 35
.

If
\iota_{I}=\iota_{K}\cdot\iota\colon I\hookrightarrow K\hookrightarrow R
are finite
residuation ideals containing
z
, then

(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\gamma_{I}(z))=(\iota_{K}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K})(\gamma_{K}(\iota(z))).

(5.5)

Proof 5.11
.

Since
I\subseteq K
, it is clear that

\displaystyle(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\gamma_{I}(z))

\displaystyle=(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\V_{x\in I}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z)

\displaystyle\leq(\iota_{K}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K})(\V_{x\in K}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z)

\displaystyle=(\iota_{K}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K})(\gamma_{K}(\iota(z))).

For the reverse inequality, note that for every join-prime

q\in\mathcal{J}K
we find
p\in\mathcal{J}I
such that
q\leq\iota(p)
, since

q\leq\top=\iota(\top)=\iota(\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}I}p)=\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}I}\iota(p)

and
q
is
join-prime. We use this to calculate

\displaystyle(\iota_{K}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K})(\gamma_{K}(\iota(z)))

\displaystyle=\V_{q\in\mathcal{J}K}\iota_{K}(q)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K}(q\setminus\iota(z))

\displaystyle=\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}I}\V_{q\leq\iota(p)}\iota_{K}(q)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K}(q\setminus\iota(z))

idempotence of join

\displaystyle=\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}I}\V_{q\leq\iota(p)}\iota_{K}(q)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I}(\iota^{*}(q)\setminus z)

\displaystyle{(*)}

\displaystyle=\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}I}\V_{q\leq\iota(p)}\iota_{I}\iota^{*}(q)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I}(\iota^{*}(q)\setminus z)

\displaystyle{(**)}

\displaystyle\leq\V_{p\in\mathcal{J}I}\iota_{I}(p)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I}(p\setminus z)

\displaystyle{({**}*)}

\displaystyle=(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\gamma_{I}(z)).

For step
(*)
, we use that for
p\in\mathcal{J}I,q\in\mathcal{J}K
such that

q\leq\iota(p)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\iota_{K}(q\setminus\iota(z))

\displaystyle=\iota_{K}(q)\setminus\iota_{K}(\iota(z))

\displaystyle=\iota_{K}(q)\setminus\iota_{I}(z)

\displaystyle=\iota_{I}(\iota_{I}^{*}(\iota_{K}(q))\setminus z)

\displaystyle\iota_{I}\text{ finite (open) residuation morphism}

\displaystyle=\iota_{I}(\iota^{*}(\iota_{K}^{*}(\iota_{K}(q)))\setminus z)

\displaystyle\iota_{I}=\iota_{K}\iota

\displaystyle=\iota_{I}(\iota^{*}(q)\setminus z)

\displaystyle\iota_{K}\text{ embedding.}

Simlarly, for step
(**)
we use have

\iota_{K}=\iota_{K}\cdot\mathrm{id}\leq\iota_{K}\cdot\iota\cdot\iota^{*}=\iota_{I}\cdot\iota^{*}.

Lastly, for step
({**}*)
note that for every
q\in\mathcal{J}K
we have

\iota^{*}(q)\in\mathcal{J}(I)
: indeed,
\iota^{*}(q)\leq x\vee y
in
I
implies

q\leq\iota(x)\vee\iota(y)
in
K
, hence
q\leq\iota(x)
or
q\leq\iota(y)
, so
\iota^{*}(q)\leq x

or
\iota^{*}(q)\leq y
. In particular for all
q\in\mathcal{J}K
we find some

p\in\mathcal{J}I
with

\iota_{I}\iota^{*}(q)\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I}(\iota^{*}(q)\setminus z)=\iota_{I}(p)\mathbin{{\otimes}}(\iota_{I}(p)\setminus z),

which implies
({**}*)
.

Now, for well-definedness of
\gamma_{\setminus}
, if
I,I^{\prime}
are finite
residuation ideals containing
z
they are both contained in a
finite residuation ideal
K
, since that
R
is locally finite; we
write
\iota\colon I\hookrightarrow K\hookleftarrow I^{\prime}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\iota^{\prime}
for the inclusion maps. Now
we have

\displaystyle(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\gamma_{I}(z))

\displaystyle=(\iota_{K}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K})(\iota\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota)(\gamma(z))

(
5.5
)

\displaystyle=(\iota_{K}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K})(\gamma_{K}(\iota(z)))

\displaystyle\iota\text{ coalgebra morphism}

\displaystyle=(\iota_{K}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{K})(\gamma_{K}(\iota^{\prime}(z)))

\displaystyle\iota,\iota^{\prime}\text{ subcoalgebras of $K$}

\displaystyle=(\iota_{I^{\prime}}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I^{\prime}})(\gamma_{I^{\prime}}(z))

backwards.

This shows that the mapping

\gamma_{\setminus}\colon R\mapsto R\mathbin{{\otimes}}R,\qquad z\mapsto(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\gamma(z))

does not depend on the choice of the residuation ideal
I
.

(1b)

We show that the mapping
\gamma_{\setminus}
indeed
yields a
U_{\land}
-coalgebra structure, that is, it preserves
finite meets. Let
F\subseteq R
be a finite subset. By local finiteness we
find a residuation ideal
I
containing
F
. Now we simply use
that both the comultiplication on
I
and
\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I}

preserve finite meets:

\gamma_{\setminus}(\A_{x\in F}x)=(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\gamma(\A_{x\in F}x))=\A_{x\in F}(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{I})(\gamma(x))=\A_{x\in F}\gamma_{\setminus}(x).

(1c)

The coalgebra is easily seen to be locally finite, since for
every finite subset
X\subseteq R
we find a finite residuation ideal
I

containing
X
, and the corresponding coalgebra structure on
I

is by definition a subcoalgebra of
(R,\gamma_{\setminus})
.

(2a)

We first show that for finite subcoalgebras
A,A^{\prime}
of
R

containing both
x,z
we have

\iota_{A}(x\setminus z)=\iota_{A^{\prime}}(x\setminus z).

First, let
\iota_{B}\cdot\iota\colon A\hookrightarrow B\hookrightarrow R
be finite
subcoalgebras. Then

\displaystyle\iota_{A}(x\setminus z)

\displaystyle=\iota_{A}(x\multimap\gamma(z))

\displaystyle=\iota_{B}(\iota(x\multimap\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle=\iota_{B}(\iota(x)\multimap(\iota\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota)(\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle\text{embeddings preserve}\multimap

\displaystyle=\iota_{B}(\iota(x)\multimap\gamma(\iota(z)))

\displaystyle\iota\text{ coalgebra morphism}

\displaystyle=\iota_{B}(\iota(x)\setminus\iota(z)).

From this it follows that

\iota_{B}(x\setminus\iota(z))=\iota_{B}(x\multimap(\iota\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota)(\gamma(z)))=\iota_{B}(\iota(\iota^{*}(x)\multimap\gamma(z)))=\iota_{A}(\iota^{*}(x)\setminus z).

(5.6)

Now let
A\hookrightarrow C
be a finite subcoalgebra containing
x
and
z
. Then

A
certainly contains the (finite) subcoalgebra
\langle z\rangle
generated
by
z
; we write
\iota\colon\langle z\rangle\hookrightarrow A
for its inclusion into

A
. We now obtain the canonical expression, where the last step
uses (
5.6
)

x\setminus_{\gamma}z=\iota_{A}(x\setminus z)=\iota_{A}(x\setminus\iota(z))=\iota_{\langle z\rangle}(\iota^{*}(x)\setminus z)

For general finite subcoalgebras
A,A^{\prime}\hookrightarrow R
containing
x,z

we find an upper bound
\iota\colon A\hookrightarrow B\hookleftarrow A^{\prime}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\iota^{\prime}
and
compute

\iota_{A}(x\setminus z)=\iota_{B}(\iota(x)\setminus\iota(z))=\iota_{B}(\iota^{\prime}(x)\setminus\iota^{\prime}(z))=\iota_{A^{\prime}}(x\setminus z).

(2b)

The proof that the residuals preserve finite meets in the
covariant component is analogous to the proof for the
comultiplication.

(2c)

The residuation algebra structure induced by
\gamma
is locally
finite: Since the coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
is locally finite, every finite
subset
F\subseteq C
is contained in a finite subcoalgebra
A\hookrightarrow C
.
The finite residuation algebra structure on
A
given by

27
(2)
makes
A
a residuation subalgebra of

(C,\setminus_{\gamma})
containing
F
.

(2d)

It remains to verify the residuation property:

\displaystyle y\leq x\setminus_{\gamma}z

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,y\leq\iota_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(x)\setminus_{z}z)

\displaystyle\text{definition }\setminus_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,y\leq\iota_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}x\multimap\gamma z)

\displaystyle\text{definition }\setminus_{z}

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\iota_{z}^{*}y\leq\iota_{z}^{*}x\multimap\gamma z

\displaystyle\iota_{z}^{*}\dashv\iota_{z}

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\iota_{z}^{*}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{z}^{*}y\leq\gamma z

\displaystyle\iota_{z}^{*}x\!\mathbin{{\otimes}}\!(-)\,\dashv\,\iota_{z}^{*}x\!\multimap\!(-)

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\iota_{z}^{*}x\leq\gamma z{\,{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}\,}\iota_{z}^{*}y

\displaystyle(-)\!\mathbin{{\otimes}}\!\iota_{z}^{*}y\,\dashv\,(-){\,{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}\,}\iota_{z}^{*}y\!

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,x\leq\iota_{z}(\gamma z{\,{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}\,}\iota_{z}^{*}y)

\displaystyle\iota_{z}^{*}\dashv\iota_{z}

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,x\leq\iota_{z}(z\mathrel{/}_{\!z}\iota_{z}^{*}y)

\displaystyle\text{definition }\mathrel{/}_{\!z}

\displaystyle\,\Longleftrightarrow\,x\leq z\mathrel{/}_{\!\gamma}y

\displaystyle\text{definition }\mathrel{/}_{\!\gamma}

(3)

The translations are inverse since they are liftings of the
translations between the operators on the finite substructures: We
first show that
\gamma_{\setminus_{\gamma}}=\gamma
.
For every
z\in R
, the
subcoalgebra
\iota_{z}\colon\langle z\rangle\hookrightarrow R
generated by
z
is a
residuation ideal of
\setminus_{\gamma}
: For all
x\in R
, we have

x\setminus_{\gamma}z=\iota_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(x)\multimap\gamma(z))\in\langle z\rangle.

We can
therefore choose it as a residuation ideal containing
z
in the
definition of
\gamma_{\setminus_{\gamma}}
to get

\gamma_{\setminus_{\gamma}}(z)=(\iota_{z}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{z})(\gamma_{\setminus_{\gamma}}(z))=(\iota_{z}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota_{z})(\gamma(z))=\gamma(z).

An analogous argument proves

\setminus_{\gamma_{\setminus}}=\setminus
.

34
shows that every locally finite
residuation algebra carries a unique
U_{\land}
-coalgebra structure and
vice versa. We may thus translate at will between the residuals and
comultiplication as in the complete case and omit the subscripts. We
extend Lemmas
28
and
29
to locally finite structures:

Lemma 36
.

Let
R
be a locally finite residuation algebra.

(1)

Finite residuation ideals correspond to finite subcoalgebras.

(2)

The residuals are associative iff the comultiplication is coassociative.

(3)

It is prime-unital iff the comultiplication is prime-counital.

(4)

The comultiplication is pure iff every finite residuation ideal is pure (see
Section 5.3
).

Proof 5.12
.

(1)

If
\iota_{I}\colon I\hookrightarrow R
is a finite residuation
ideal, then by definition its comultiplication makes
I
a
subcoalgebra of
(R,\gamma)
.

In the reserve direction, let
A\hookrightarrow R
be a
finite subcoalgebra.
The residuation algebra structure on
A
due to

27
(1)
together with the definition

x\setminus z=\iota_{A}(x\setminus z)
of the residuation algebra structure on
R

show that
A
is a
residuation subalgebra of
R
.
To show that
A
is a residuation ideal, let
x\in R
and
z\in A
.
There exists a finite subcoalgebra
B
containing
x,z
; we denote the inclusion map by
\iota\colon A\hookrightarrow B
.
By (
5.6
) we then have

x\setminus z=\iota_{B}(x\setminus z)=\iota_{A}(\iota^{*}(x)\setminus z),

which states that
x\setminus z
lies in
A
.

(2)

First, let
\gamma
be the coassociative comultiplication, and let
x,y,z\in R
.
By local finiteness these elements are contained in a finite coassociative subcoalgebra
\iota_{A}\colon A\hookrightarrow R
.
Then by
36
(1)
,
\iota_{A}
, is an associative finite residuation ideal of
R
, whence

x\setminus(z\mathrel{/}y)=\iota_{A}(x\setminus(z\mathrel{/}y))=\iota_{A}((x\setminus z)\mathrel{/}y)=(x\setminus z)\mathrel{/}y.

The other direction works analogously: if the residuals are associative and
z\in R
, then
it is contained in a finite associative residuation ideal
\iota_{I}\colon I\hookrightarrow R
.
So
I
is a finite associative subcoalgebra of
R
and we have

\displaystyle(\bar{\gamma}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})(\bar{\gamma}(\iota_{I}(z)))

\displaystyle=(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\iota_{I})((\bar{\gamma}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})(\bar{\gamma}(z)))

\displaystyle=(\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\iota_{I})((\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\bar{\gamma})(\bar{\gamma}(z)))

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\bar{\gamma})(\bar{\gamma}(\iota_{I}(z))),

proving that
\bar{\gamma}
is associative.

(3)

Let
\epsilon\colon R\rightarrow 2
be a counit for the comultiplication

\bar{\gamma}=\omega\cdot\gamma\colon U_{\land}R\rightarrow U_{\land}(R\mathbin{{\otimes}}R)\cong U_{\land}R\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}U_{\land}R

with left adjoint element
e\in\mathbf{JSL}(2,R)\cong R
.
Note that
\epsilon\cdot\iota_{A}
is a counit for every subcoalgebra
\iota_{A}\colon A\hookrightarrow R
:
Let
z\in A
, then, since
\epsilon
is a unit for
\bar{\gamma}
, we have

\iota_{A}(((\epsilon\cdot\iota_{A})\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id}_{A})(\bar{\gamma}(z)))=(\epsilon\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})(\iota_{A}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\iota_{A})(\bar{\gamma}(z))=(\epsilon\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})(\bar{\gamma}(\iota_{A}(z)))=\iota_{A}(z).

But by the implication
(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)
of
29
this means that the left adjoint
e_{A}:=\iota_{A}^{*}(e)\in A
of
\epsilon\cdot\iota_{A}
is a unit for the finite residuation ideal
A
of
R
.
So for all
z\in R
we have

e\setminus z=\iota_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(e)\setminus z)=\iota_{z}(e_{z}\setminus z)=\iota_{z}(z)=z,

so
e
is a unit for the residuals.
The case for the right residual
\mathrel{/}
is dual.

For the other direction let
e\in R
be a unit for the residuals with right adjoint
\epsilon\colon R\rightarrow 2
.
For every residuation ideal
\iota_{I}\colon I\hookrightarrow R
the element
\iota_{I}^{*}(e)\in I
is the unit of
I
:
The embedding trivially is an open residuation morphism,
and we have

\iota_{I}(\iota_{I}^{*}(e)\setminus z)=e\setminus\iota_{I}(z)=\iota_{I}(z)\qquad\text{for every $z\in I$}.

So the subcoalgebra structure
I
has the counit

\epsilon\cdot\iota_{I}
. Now given
z\in R
, we pick a
residuation ideal
I
containing
z
using that
R
is
locally finite. Then we have

\displaystyle(\epsilon\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})(\bar{\gamma}(z))

\displaystyle=(\epsilon\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})(\bar{\gamma}(\iota_{I}(z)))

\displaystyle=(\epsilon\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})((\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\iota_{I})(\bar{\gamma}(z)))

\displaystyle=\iota_{I}((\epsilon\iota_{I}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{id})(\bar{\gamma}(z)))

\displaystyle=\iota_{I}(z)=z.

This shows that
\epsilon
is a counit for
\bar{\gamma}
.

(4)

If the comultiplication is pure, associative and has a counit, then this holds for every finite subcoalgebra.
Every finite residuation ideal
I
of
R
is a finite pure subcoalgebra, which therefore is a derivation algebra.

Conversely, if every finite residuation ideal is a derivation algebra, then we only have to show that the comultiplication preserves finite joins, since it already is coassociative and has a counit.
The join of finitely many elements
is taken in some finite subcoalgebra
A
.
But
A
is a finite residuation ideal and therefore by assumption a derivation algebra, and the comultiplication preserves finite joins.

Remark 37
.

36
(4)
characterizes locally finite
residuation algebras with a pure comultiplication. By extended
duality, its dual Priestley relation is functional.
Gehrke
[
Geh16b
, Prop. 3.15]
has presented a necessary and
sufficient condition for a general residuation algebra
R
to have
a functional dual relation, namely
join-preservation at
primes
:

\forall F\in\mathbf{DL}(R,2)\colon\forall a\in F\colon\forall b,c\in R\colon\exists a^{\prime}\in F\colon a\setminus(b\lor c)\leq(a^{\prime}\setminus b)\lor(a^{\prime}\setminus c).

One can indeed show that every locally finite residuation algebra with a pure comultiplication (
36
(4)
) is join-preserving at primes:
If
F
is a prime filter on
C
and
a\in F
, then we obtain by local finiteness for all
b,c\in C
a finite
pure
residuation ideal
\iota\colon I\hookrightarrow C
containing
a,b,c
.
In this residuation ideal we have
a=\V K
for join-primes
K\subseteq\mathcal{J}I\subseteq C
.
Since
F
is prime, some
a^{\prime}\in K
lies in
F
, and it satisfies

\displaystyle a\setminus(b\lor c)

\displaystyle=\iota(a\setminus(b\lor c))

\displaystyle=\iota((\V K)\setminus(b\lor c))

\displaystyle\leq\iota(a^{\prime}\setminus(b\lor c))

(-)\setminus x
antimonotone

\displaystyle=\iota(a^{\prime}\setminus b\lor a^{\prime}\setminus c)

I
is pure

\displaystyle=\iota(a^{\prime}\setminus b)\lor\iota(a^{\prime}\setminus c)

\displaystyle=(a^{\prime}\setminus b)\lor(a^{\prime}\setminus c)

\displaystyle a^{\prime},b,c\in I.

This shows that the residuals are join-preserving at primes.

{defi}

A residuation algebra
R
is a
derivation
algebra
if it is locally finite, associative, prime-unital and
every finite residuation ideal
I
is pure.
This yields full subcategories

\mathbf{Der}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Res}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{RelRes}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{RelDer}.

Note that a derivation algebra with a finite carrier is precisely a finite derivation ACDL as defined in
Section 5.3
(2)
. Recall from
Section 5.3
the definition morphism for
U_{\land}
-coalgebras and comonoids.

Proposition 38
.

Let
R,R^{\prime}
be locally finite residuation algebras with units.

(1)

A lattice morphism
f\in\mathbf{DL}(R,R^{\prime})
is a residuation morphism iff it is a prime-counital morphism of
U_{\land}
-coalgebras.

(2)

If
R,R^{\prime}
are comonoids, a finite join-preserving function
\rho\in\mathbf{JSL}(R,R^{\prime})
is a corelational residuation morphism iff it is a corelational comonoid morphism.

Proof 5.13
.

(1)

First, let
f\colon R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
be a residuation morphism.
For
z\in R
we choose a finite residuation ideal
I^{\prime}\hookrightarrow R^{\prime}
containing
f(z)
.
Since
f
is a residuation morphism we have by the (
Back
) condition that for every
y\in I^{\prime}
there exists some
x_{y,z}
with
y\leq f(x_{y,z})
and
y\setminus f(z)=f(x_{y,z}\setminus z)
.
We now choose a finite ideal
I\hookrightarrow R
containing
z
and all
x_{y,z}
for
y\in I^{\prime}
.
We therefore have

\displaystyle\gamma_{\setminus}(f(z))

\displaystyle=\V_{y\in I^{\prime}}y\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\setminus f(z)

\displaystyle\leq\V_{y\in I^{\prime},y\leq f(x_{y,z})}f(x_{y,z})\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(x_{y,z}\setminus z)

\displaystyle=(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f)(\V_{y\in I^{\prime},y\leq f(x_{y,z})}x_{y,z}\mathbin{{\otimes}}x_{y,z}\setminus z)

\displaystyle\leq(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f)(\V_{x\in I}x\mathbin{{\otimes}}x\setminus z)

\displaystyle=(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f)(\gamma_{\setminus}(z)).

For the reverse inequality let
z\in R
be contained in the finite residuation ideal
I
.
We choose a finite residuation ideal
J\hookrightarrow R^{\prime}
containing
f(z)
and all
f(x),x\in I
, and use (
Forth
):

\displaystyle(f\mathbin{{\otimes}}f)(\gamma_{\setminus}(z))

\displaystyle=\V_{x\in I}f(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(x\setminus z)

\displaystyle\leq\V_{x\in I}f(x)\mathbin{{\otimes}}f(x)\setminus f(z)

\displaystyle\leq\V_{y\in J}y\mathbin{{\otimes}}y\setminus f(z)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\setminus}(f(z)),

This proves that
f
is a morphism of
U_{\land}
-coalgebras.

Conversely, let
f\colon R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
be a morphism of
U_{\land}
-coalgebras.
For every
z\in R
the morphism
f
restricts to the finite subcoalgebras generated by
z,f(z)
as
f_{z}\colon\langle z\rangle\rightarrow\langle f(z)\rangle
.
If we denote the respective inclusions by
\iota_{z}\colon\langle z\rangle\hookrightarrow R
and
\iota_{f(z)}\colon\langle f(z)\rangle\hookrightarrow R^{\prime}
, then this is equivalent to saying that
f\cdot\iota_{z}=\iota_{f(z)}\cdot f_{z}
.
From the unit of
\iota_{z}
we thus get

f\leq f\cdot\iota_{z}\cdot\iota_{z}^{*}=\iota_{f(z)}\cdot f_{z}\cdot\iota_{z}^{*},

which under transposition is equivalent to

\iota_{f(z)}^{*}\cdot f\leq f_{z}\cdot\iota_{z}^{*}.

(5.7)

This entails the (
Forth
) condition:

\displaystyle f(x\setminus_{\gamma}z)

\displaystyle=f(\iota_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(x)\multimap\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle\text{def. }\setminus_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\iota_{f(z)}(f_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(x)\multimap\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle f_{z}\text{ restriction}

\displaystyle\leq\iota_{f(z)}(f_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(x))\multimap(f_{z}\mathbin{{\otimes}}f_{z})\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle\multimap\text{\autoref{prop:mon-impl}\ref{prop:mon-impl:preserve}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{(z)}(f_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(x))\multimap\gamma(f_{z}(z)))

\displaystyle f_{z}\text{ coalgbra morphism}

\displaystyle\leq\iota_{f(z)}(\iota^{*}_{f(z)}(f(x))\multimap\gamma(f_{z}(z)))

(
5.7
) + contravariance

\displaystyle\leq\iota_{f(z)}(\iota^{*}_{f(z)}(f(x))\multimap\gamma(f(z)))

\displaystyle f_{z}(z)=f(z)

\displaystyle=f(x)\setminus_{\gamma}f(z).

To verify the (
Back
) condition, let
y\in R^{\prime},z\in R
and put

x_{y,z}=\iota_{z}(f_{z}^{*}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y))).

Then

y\leq\iota_{f(z)}\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y)\leq\iota_{f(z)}(f_{z}(f_{z}^{*}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y))))=f(\iota_{z}(f_{z}^{*}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y))))=f(x_{y,z})

and

\displaystyle y\setminus_{\gamma}f(z)

\displaystyle=\iota_{f(z)}(\iota^{*}_{f(z)}(y)\multimap\gamma(f_{z}(z)))

\displaystyle\text{def. }\setminus_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\iota_{f(z)}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y)\multimap(f_{z}\mathbin{{\otimes}}f_{z})(\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle f_{z}\text{ coalgebra morphism}

\displaystyle=\iota_{f(z)}(f_{z}(f_{z}^{*}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y))\multimap\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle=f(\iota_{z}(f^{*}_{z}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*})\multimap\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle\iota_{f(z)}\cdot f_{z}=f\cdot\iota_{z}

\displaystyle=f(\iota_{z}(\iota_{z}^{*}(\iota_{z}(f_{z}^{*}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y))))\multimap\gamma(z)))

\displaystyle\iota_{z}^{*}\iota_{z}=\mathrm{id}

\displaystyle=f_{z}(x_{y,z}\setminus_{\gamma}z)

\displaystyle\text{def. }x_{y,z}.

For the prime-(co-)unitality conditions we split the pointwise equality
\forall x\colon\epsilon^{\prime}(f(x))=\epsilon(x)
into
\epsilon^{\prime}(f(x))\leq\epsilon(x)
and
\epsilon(x)\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(f(x))
.
These are equivalent to
e^{\prime}\leq f(x)\Rightarrow e\leq x
and
e\leq x\Rightarrow e^{\prime}\leq f(x)
, respectively, combining to the desired condition
\forall x\colon e^{\prime}\leq f(x)\Leftrightarrow e\leq x
.

(2)

Now let
R,R^{\prime}
be comonoids and let
f\in\mathbf{JSL}(R,R^{\prime})
be a corelational comonoid morphism.
For
x,z\in R
we choose a finite subcomonoid
\iota\colon I\hookrightarrow R
that contains
x,z
and a finite subcomonoid
\iota^{\prime}\colon I^{\prime}\hookrightarrow R^{\prime}
(with comultiplication
\gamma^{\prime}
) containing
\rho[I]
.
Then
\rho
restricts to a corelational morphism
\rho\colon I\rightarrow I^{\prime}
of finite comonoids. By
31

\rho
is a corelational morphism of finite residuation algebras, so it satisfies
\rho(a\setminus_{\gamma}b)\leq\rho(a)\setminus_{\gamma^{\prime}}\rho(b)
for all
a,b\in I
.
We therefore get

\rho(x\setminus_{\gamma}z)=\rho(\iota(x\setminus_{\gamma}z))=\iota^{\prime}(\rho(x\setminus_{\gamma}z))\leq\iota^{\prime}(\rho(x)\setminus_{\gamma^{\prime}}\rho(z))=\rho(x)\setminus_{\gamma^{\prime}}(z).

which proves that
f
is a corelational residuation morphism.
To verify
e^{\prime}\leq\rho(e)
we again choose finite subcoalgebras
A,A^{\prime}
with
e\in A
and
\rho[A]\cup\{e^{\prime}\}\subseteq A^{\prime}
.
Since
\rho
is prime-counital it satisfies
\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\rho
and therefore also
\epsilon\cdot\iota_{A}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\rho\cdot\iota_{A}=\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\iota_{A^{\prime}}\cdot\rho
.
As
\epsilon\cdot\iota_{A}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\iota_{A^{\prime}}
are the counits for
A
and
A^{\prime}
, respectively, its restriction
\rho\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is thus also prime-counital and whence satisfies
\iota_{A}^{*}(e)\leq\rho(\iota_{A^{\prime}}^{*}(e^{\prime}))
for the corresponding units of the residuals on
A,A^{\prime}
.
But
e\in A
and
e^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
, so this equation simplifies to the desired
e\leq\rho(e^{\prime})
.

Conversely, if
f
is a corelational residuation morphism choose for
z\in R

a finite residuation ideal
\iota\colon I\hookrightarrow R
containing
z
and a finite residuation ideal
\iota^{\prime}\colon I^{\prime}\hookrightarrow R
containing
\rho[I]
.
Then we have

\displaystyle(\rho\mathbin{{\otimes}}\rho)(\gamma(z))

\displaystyle=(\rho\mathbin{{\otimes}}\rho)(\gamma(\iota(z)))

\displaystyle=(\rho\mathbin{{\otimes}}\rho)(\iota\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota)(\gamma(z))

\displaystyle=(\iota^{\prime}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota^{\prime})(\rho\mathbin{{\otimes}}\rho)(\gamma(z))

\displaystyle\leq(\iota^{\prime}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\iota^{\prime})\gamma(\rho(z))

\displaystyle=\gamma(\rho(z)),

so
\rho
is a corelational residuation morphism.
To show that
\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\rho
, take
z\in R
with ideals
I,I^{\prime}
chosen as before.
Recall that
\iota^{*}(e)
is a unit of the residuation ideal
I
and
\epsilon\cdot\iota
is a counit for the corresponding subcoalgebra.
Since
\rho
is prime-unital it satisfies

e^{\prime}\leq\rho(e)\leq\rho(\iota(\iota^{*}(e)))=\iota^{\prime}(\rho(\iota^{*}(e)))

which is equivalent to
\iota^{\prime*}(e^{\prime})\leq\rho(\iota^{*}(e))
.
Since
\rho\colon I\rightarrow I^{\prime}
is a corelational residuation morphism it is a corelational morphism of the coalgebra structures on
I,I^{\prime}
and we thus get

\epsilon(z)=\epsilon(\iota(z))\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(\iota^{\prime}(\rho(z)))=\epsilon^{\prime}(\rho(\iota(z)))=\epsilon^{\prime}(\rho(z)).

Remark 39
.

The proof of
38
gives an alternative formulation of the (
Back
) condition for locally finite residuation algebras as

y\setminus f(z)=f((\iota_{z}\cdot f_{z}^{*}\cdot\iota_{f(z)}^{*})(y)\setminus z).

Here we choose the existentially quantified
x_{y,z}
in (
Back
) via
f_{z}^{*}

locally
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{y,z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(x_{y,z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\setminus{z}/}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\setminus{f(z)}/}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{z}^{*}(\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f(z)}^{*}(y)}

\in

\in

\geq

\in

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{z}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f(z)}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{z}}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{z}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f(z)}}

\in

\in

Compare this with Equation (
Open
) satisfied by open residuation morphisms,
where the existence of a global left adjoint
f^{*}
allows one to
choose
x_{y,z}=f^{*}(y)

globally
, that is, independently of
z
.

We then have the following extension of
31
to locally finite structures:

Theorem 40
.

(1)

The category of
locally finite residuation algebras and residuation morphisms is isomorphic
to the category of locally finite prime-unital
U_{\land}
-coalgebras and
pure coalgebra morphisms.

(2)

This isomorphism restricts to an isomorphism between the
full subcategories of derivation algebras and locally finite comonoids.

(3)

The category of
derivation algebras and corelational residuation morphisms is isomorphic to the
category of locally finite comonoids with relational
morphisms.

Proof 5.14
.

Immediate from
36
and
38
.

5.5.
Duality Theory for Locally Finite Residuation Algebras

We now gathered the ingredients to present the first application of our abstract extended duality (
2
): a categorical duality between
profinite ordered monoids
and

derivation algebras
.
Recall that a profinite ordered monoid is a codirected limit of finite ordered monoids; like in the order-discrete setting, they are equivalent to
Priestley monoids
, viz. monoids in the cartesian category
\mathbf{Priest}
(
45
).
This result is a non-trivial
restriction of Gehrke’s duality
[
Geh16a
,
Geh16b
]

between Priestley-topological algebras and residuation algebras.

Conceptually, this general duality is an extension of the finite duality
\mathbf{OrdMon}_{\mathrm{f}}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{f}}\cong\mathbf{Der}_{\mathrm{f}}
by forming suitable completions. We
start by investigating the Ind- and Pro-completions of the categories involved in the finite
duality
33
.

Remark 41
.

The
Ind-completion
(or
free completion under filtered colimits
) of a small category
\mathbf{C}
is given by a category
\ind(\mathbf{C})
with filtered (equivalently directed) colimits and a full embedding
I\colon\mathbf{C}\hookrightarrow\ind(\mathbf{C})
such that every functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
into a category
\mathbf{D}
with filtered colimits extends to a functor
\overline{F}\colon\ind(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{D}
, unique up to natural isomorphism, such that
F=\overline{F}\cdot I
. To show that a category
\mathbf{D}
is the Ind-completion of a full subcategory
\mathbf{C}
, it suffices to prove the following (see e.g.
[
ACMU21
, Thm. A.4]
):

(1)

\mathbf{D}
has filtered colimits,

(2)

every object of
\mathbf{D}
is a filtered colimit of objects of
\mathbf{C}
, and

(3)

every object
C
of
\mathbf{C}
is finitely presentable in
\mathbf{D}
, that is, the functor
\mathbf{D}(C,-)\colon\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{Set}
preserves filtered colimits.

The
Pro-completion
(or
free completion under cofiltered limits
) of
\mathbf{C}
is defined dually.

Profinite ordered monoids form the Pro-completion of the category of finite ordered monoids.
Dually, lattice comonoids (and therefore also derivation algebras by
40
(2)
) form Ind-completions of their respective subcategories of finite objects:

Proposition 42
.

The category of locally finite comonoids forms the Ind-completion of the category of finite comonoids:

\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{lf}}\simeq\ind(\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{f}}).

Proof 5.15
.

(a) We first show that filtered colimits of lattice comonoids are formed in
\mathbf{Set}
.
First, since
\mathbf{DL}
is a category of algebras over a finitary signature, filtered colimits in
\mathbf{DL}
are formed in
\mathbf{Set}
.
Second, since
+\colon\mathbf{DL}\times\mathbf{DL}\rightarrow\mathbf{DL}
is a finitary functor (colimits commute with colimits) filtered colimits in the category of
+
-coalgebras are also formed in
\mathbf{Set}
.
As comonoids are a full subcategory of
+
-coalgebras it suffices to show that the filtered colimit in
+
-coalgebras of lattice comonoids is again a comonoid, which is a straightforward verification:
Let
d_{i}\colon D_{i}\rightarrow D_{i}+D_{i},i\in I
be a cofiltered diagram of comonoids, and let
d\colon D\rightarrow D+D
be their colimit in the category of
+
-coalgebras with colimit injections
\kappa_{i}\colon D_{i}\rightarrow D
.
Since the colimit
D
is formed in
\mathbf{Set}
, there exists for every
x\in D
some
i\in I
and
x_{i}\in D_{i}
with
\kappa_{i}(x_{i})=x
.
But then

\displaystyle(d+\mathrm{id})(d(x))

\displaystyle=(d+\mathrm{id})d(\kappa_{i}(x_{i}))

\displaystyle=(d+\mathrm{id})(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i})(d_{i}(x_{i}))

\kappa_{i}
comonoid morphism

\displaystyle=((d\cdot\kappa_{i})+\kappa_{i})(d_{i}(x_{i}))

\displaystyle=(((\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i})\cdot d_{i})+\kappa_{i})(d_{i}(x_{i}))

\kappa_{i}
comonoid morphism

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i})(d_{i}+\mathrm{id})(d_{i}(x_{i}))

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i})(\mathrm{id}+d_{i})(d_{i}(x_{i}))

\displaystyle d_{i}\text{ comonoid}

\displaystyle=\ \cdots

backwards

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}+d)(d(x)),

so
D
is coassociative.
Counitality works similarly for
\epsilon\colon D\rightarrow 2
with
\epsilon(x)=\epsilon(\kappa_{i}(x_{i}))=\epsilon_{i}(x_{i})
.
This proves that filtered colimits of comonoids are formed in
\mathbf{Set}
.

(b) To prove that the category
\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{lf}}
is the Ind-completion of its full subcategory
\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{f}}
, we verify the conditions of
41
:

(1)

\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{lf}}
has filtered colimits: filtered colimits of comonoids are formed in
\mathbf{Set}
by (a). Moreover, a filtered colimit
c_{i}\colon C_{i}\to C
(
i\in I
) of locally finite comonoids is locally finite: Given
x\in C
one has
x=c_{i}(x_{i})
for some
i\in I
and
x_{i}\in C_{i}
. Thus
x_{i}\in C_{i}^{\prime}
for some finite subcomonoid
C_{i}^{\prime}
of
C_{i}
, and so
x_{i}
lies in the finite subcomonoid
c_{i}[C_{i}^{\prime}]\subseteq C
.

(2)

Every locally finite comonoid is the directed union of the diagram of all its finite subcomonoids. This follows again from (a), since this is clearly a directed union in
\mathbf{Set}
.

(3)

Every finite comonoid is finitely presentable: A finite comonoid can be regarded as a coalgebra
C\to 2\times(C+C)
for the functor
FX=2\times(X+X)
on
\mathbf{DL}
by pairing its counit and comultiplication. Every finite
F
-coalgebra is finitely presentable in the category of all
F
-coalgebras
[
AP04
, Lemma 3.2]
. This implies the corresponding statement for finite comonoids since they form a full subcategory of the category of
F
-coalgebras.

On the dual side, we require a property of Priestley monoids (
45
below), namely that every Priestley monoid is profinite, that is, it is a cofiltered limit of finite ordered monoids with the discrete topology. The corresponding result for (unordered) Stone monoids is well known
[
Joh82
, Thm. VI.2.9]
; its ordered version is analogous, and we give a full proof for the convenience of the reader.

We need some auxiliary results:
First, we recall a well-known characterization for quotients of ordered algebras, see e.g.
[
MU19
, Sec. B.2]
.
Recall that a preorder
\sqsubseteq
on an ordered monoid
A
is

stable
if
\sqsubseteq
refines the order on
A

(i.e.
\leq
is included in
\sqsubseteq
) and the multiplication
is monotone:
a\sqsubseteq b,a^{\prime}\sqsubseteq b^{\prime}
implies
aa^{\prime}\sqsubseteq bb^{\prime}
.

Lemma 43
.

Let
X
be an ordered monoid.
Then ordered monoid quotients of
X
are in bijective correspondence with stable preorders on
X
.

Proof 5.16
.

A quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
induces a stable preorder by putting
a\sqsubseteq a^{\prime}
iff
e(a)\leq e(a^{\prime})
for
a,a^{\prime}\in A
.
Conversely, from a stable preorder
\sqsubseteq
on
A\times A
we obtain an equivalence relation on
A
by identifying
a\sim a^{\prime}
iff
a\sqsubseteq a^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime}\sqsubseteq a
.
Monotonicity of the multiplication then ensures that multiplication on equivalence classes is well-defined.
The refinement property ensures that the canonical projection, which maps an element to its equivalence class, is order-preserving.
It is a routine verification to check that these constructions are inverses.

Lemma 44
.

Let
X
be a Priestley monoid. If
x\not\leq y
, then there exists a finite Priestley monoid quotient
f\colon X\twoheadrightarrow M
such that
fx\not\leq fy
.

We modify the proof of a corresponding statement for Stone algebras given by Johnstone
[
Joh82
, Ch. VI, Sec. 2.7]
.

Proof 5.17
.

Let
x\not\leq y
be elements of a Priestley monoid
X
, we show that there exists a quotient of
f\colon X\twoheadrightarrow M
into some finite discretely-topologized ordered monoid
M
satisfying
f(x)\not\leq f(y)
.
Since the underlying Priestley space of
X
is profinite, there exists a continuous surjection
e\in\mathbf{Priest}(X,A)
such that
A
is a finite discretely topologized poset, satisfying
e(x)\not\leq e(y)
.
We denote the preorder on
X
induced by
e
by
\sqsubseteq
(i.e.
x\sqsubseteq y
iff
e(x)\leq e(y)
).
We define
\preceq
by

x\preceq y\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall u,v\in X\colon uxv\sqsubseteq uyv,

and denote its corresponding equivalence relation by
\approx
.

(1) We prove that
\preceq
is a stable preorder.
First we show that
\preceq
refines
\leq
: if
x\leq y
, then
\forall u,v\in X\colon uxv\leq uyv
since the multiplication is monotone. Moreover, we have
uxv\sqsubseteq uyv
for all
u,v\in X
, since
\leq
is contained in
\sqsubseteq
.
It follows that multiplication is
\preceq
-monotone: if
x\preceq y
and
x^{\prime}\preceq y^{\prime}
, then for all
u,v\in X
we have

u(xx^{\prime})v=ux(x^{\prime}v)\sqsubseteq uy(x^{\prime}v)=(uy)x^{\prime}v\sqsubseteq(uy)y^{\prime}v=u(yy^{\prime})v,

whence
xx^{\prime}\preceq yy^{\prime}
.

(2a) We prove that the equivalence relation
{\approx}\subseteq X\times X
is open.
The equivalence relation induced by
\sqsubseteq
is denoted by
\equiv
, that is,
x\equiv y
iff
x\sqsubseteq y\sqsubseteq x
iff
e(x)=e(y)
.
Note that
\equiv
is open since it is the preimage of the (open) diagonal of
A
under
e\times e
.
Now let
m\approx n
. By openness of
\equiv
and continuity of the multiplication we obtain for all
u,v\in X
open neighbourhoods
U_{u},U_{v},V_{u,v}
and
W_{u,v}
of
u,v,m,n
, respectively, such that

\displaystyle\forall u^{\prime}\in U_{u},v^{\prime}\in U_{v},m^{\prime}\in V_{u,v},n^{\prime}\in W_{u,v}\colon u^{\prime}m^{\prime}v^{\prime}\equiv u^{\prime}n^{\prime}v^{\prime}.

(5.8)

By compactness of
X
we have
X\times X=\bigcup_{u,v}U_{u}\times U_{v}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{u_{i}}\times U_{v_{i}}
for some
n
and
u_{i},v_{i}
.
Now set
V=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}V_{u_{i},v_{i}}
and
W=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}W_{u_{i},v_{i}}
. Then

V\times W
is an open neighbourhood of
(m,n)
satisfying
V\times W\subseteq{\approx}
:
for
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})\in V\times W
we have for all
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in X
some
i
with
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in U_{u_{i}}\times U_{v_{i}}
. Using (
5.8
) we have
u^{\prime}m^{\prime}v^{\prime}\equiv u^{\prime}n^{\prime}v^{\prime}
, proving
m^{\prime}\approx n^{\prime}
.

(2b) We prove that every equivalence class
[x]_{\approx}
is open.
For every
y\in[x]_{\approx}
, there exists, since
\approx
is open by (2a), a basic open
U\times V\subseteq\,\,\approx
with
(x,y)\in U\times V
.
For every
y^{\prime}\in V
we have
(x,y^{\prime})\in U\times V\subseteq{\approx}
, so
V
is an open neighbourhood of
y
that is contained in
[x]_{\approx}
, proving that
\approx
is open.

(3) It follows that the quotient
f
induced by
\preceq
has the desired properties:
(3a) It is a homomorphism of ordered monoids by
43
, since
\preceq
is a stable preorder.
(3c) The codomain
X/{\approx}
is finite and discrete, since equivalence relations with open equivalence classes are discrete, so
X/{\approx}
is finite by compactness.
(3d) Finally we show that
f
order-separates
x
and
y
:
For the sake of contradiction suppose
f(x)\leq f(y)
.
By definition we have
x\preceq y
, implying
x=1x1\sqsubseteq 1y1=y
,
a contradiction.

Proposition 45
.

Every Priestley monoid is profinite:

\mathbf{PriestMon}\cong\mathbf{ProfOrdMon}.

Proof 5.18
.

For every
X\in\mathbf{PriestMon}
we prove that
X\cong\lim D_{X}
, where
D_{X}
is the canonical codirected diagram for
X
over all finite discretely-topologized ordered monoid quotients of
X
; in particular, this shows that
X
is profinite.

First note that the limit of a diagram
D
in
\mathbf{PriestMon}
is formed by taking the limit
L=\lim|D|
of the underlying diagram
|-|\cdot D
in
\mathbf{StoneMon}
and equipping it with the product order. Then all projections are monotone, and
this order makes
L
totally order-disconnected:
if
(x_{e})_{e},(y_{e})_{e}\in L
with
(x_{e})_{e}\not\leq(y_{e})_{e}
then there exists by definition of the order some
quotient
e\colon X\twoheadrightarrow X_{e}

with
x_{e}\not\leq y_{e}
.
Hence
p_{e}^{-1}[{\uparrow}x_{e}]
is a clopen upset of
L
containing
(x_{e})_{e}
but not
(y_{e})_{e}
.
This shows that
L
is a Priestley monoid, and it is easy to verify that it satisfies the universal property in
\mathbf{PriestMon}
.

We have to prove that the canonical homomorphism
\phi\colon X\rightarrow\lim D_{X}
is an isomorphism of Priestley monoids, or equivalently, that it is a continuous surjective order-embedding. Continuity and order-preservation are given since
\phi
is a morphism in
\mathbf{Priest}
.

That
\phi
is an order-embedding follows from it being order-reflecting:
\phi(x)\leq\phi(y)\Rightarrow x\leq y
.
By contraposition it suffices to show
x\not\leq y\Rightarrow\phi(x)\not\leq\phi(y)
, but the right side of the implication is equivalent to finding an ordered monoid quotient
f\colon X\twoheadrightarrow A
into a finite Priestley monoid with
f(x)\not\leq f(y)
.
This is precisely the content of
44
.

To show that
\phi
is surjective, we use a general property of codirected limits of compact Hausdorff spaces
[
RZ10
, Lemma 1.1.5]
: if
f_{i}\colon X\twoheadrightarrow X_{i}
is a compatible family of surjections into a codirected diagram
X_{i}
, then the induced mapping
f\colon X\rightarrow\lim X_{i}
is surjective.

By duality we immediately obtain the following result:

Corollary 46
.

Every comonoid is locally finite:
\mathbf{Comon}\;\cong\;\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{lf}}
.

{defi}

Let
X
and
Y
be Priestley monoids.
A
Priestley relational morphism

X\rightarrow Y
is a total Priestley relation

\rho\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}Y
such that

\rho(x)\rho(x^{\prime})\subseteq\rho(xx^{\prime})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad 1_{N}\in\rho(1_{M}).

Theorem 47
.

(1)

The category of derivation algebras is
dually equivalent to the category of Priestley monoids

\mathbf{Der}\;\cong\;\mathbf{Comon}\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\mathbf{PriestMon}.

(2)

The duality from
item (1)
extends to Priestley relational morphisms:

\mathbf{RelDer}\;\cong\;\mathbf{RelComon}\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\mathbf{RelPriestMon}.

Proof 5.19
.

(1)

We assemble all the steps of the (dual) equivalences:
The category of profinite ordered monoids is the Pro-completion of the category of finite ordered monoids
[
ACMU21
, Prop. 2.10]
.
Since the category of finite ordered monoids is dual to the category of finite comonoids (
32
restricted to finite structures), the Pro-completion of the former is dual to the Ind-completion of the latter. By
42
,
the latter is equivalent to the category of locally finite comonoids – but this category is, by
40
(2)
, equivalent to the category of derivation algebras:

\mathbf{PriestMon}\;\simeq\;\pro(\mathbf{OrdMon}_{\mathrm{f}})\;\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\;\ind(\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{f}})\;\simeq\;\mathbf{Comon}_{\mathrm{lf}}\;\simeq\;\mathbf{Der}.

(2)

A Priestley relational morphism from
M
to
N
is precisely a total Priestley relation
\rho\colon M\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}N
such that the following diagrams commute laxly as indicated.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}N\times\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(N\times N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\times\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\delta}}

\leq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}(\cdot_{N})}

\leq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

Recall that
\mathbb{V}_{\!\downarrow}\cong\widehat{F}_{\lor}\hat{U}_{\lor}
for
U_{\lor}\colon\mathbf{DL}\rightarrow\mathbf{JSL}
, so
under extended duality
\rho
dualizes precisely to a corelational morphism of comonoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}\hat{M}\mathbin{{\otimes}}U_{\lor}\hat{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}\hat{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}\hat{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}\hat{N}\mathbin{{\otimes}}U_{\lor}\hat{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}\hat{N}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}\hat{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}(\hat{\cdot}_{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}(\hat{1}_{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\rho}^{-}\mathbin{{\otimes}}\hat{\rho}^{-}}

\leq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\rho}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}(\hat{\cdot}_{M})}

\leq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\rho}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lor}(\hat{1}_{N})}

Together with
40
(3)
this extends the duality established in
item (1)
.

Remark 48
.

47
restricts to a duality between the category of profinite monoids and Stone relational morphisms and the category of Boolean derivation algebras and corelational residuation morphisms.

6.
Duality for the Category of Small Categories

As a final application of the abstract extended duality framework, we derive a concrete description of
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}}
, the dual of the category of small categories and functors.
We instantiate the parameters of
Section 3
to the Diagram (
5.1
).

It is well known that small categories can be described in an object-free way as partial monoids
[
ML98
,
FJSZ23
,
SS67
]
with an additional
locality
condition.
For our purposes it will be convenient to describe partial monoids more generally as monoids in the monoidal category
\mathbf{Rel}
.

{nota}

Given a ternary relation
r\colon X\times Y\rightarrow\mathcal{P}Z
, we obtain the relation

\bigcup{\cdot}\mathcal{P}(r)\cdot\hat{\delta}\colon\mathcal{P}X\times\mathcal{P}Y\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}Z\rightarrow\mathcal{P}Z,\qquad(A,B)\mapsto\bigcup_{x\in A,y\in B}r(x,y).

(6.1)

Abusing notation, we denote the map (
6.1
) also by
r\colon\mathcal{P}X\times\mathcal{P}Y\rightarrow\mathcal{P}Z
.
We write
r_{@}(x,y)
if
r(x,y)\neq\emptyset
, and similarly
x\mathrel{r_{@}}y
if
r
is used as an infix operator.
Furthermore, we identify singleton sets
\{x\}\subseteq X
with their unique inhabitants
x\in X
.

{defi}

(1)

A
relational monoid
consists of a carrier set
M
, a subset
E\hookrightarrow M
of
identities
and a
multiplication relation

\circ\colon M\times M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}M
such that the following diagrams commute in
\mathbf{Rel}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\bar{\times}M\bar{\times}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\bar{\times}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\bar{\times}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\bar{\times}\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\bar{\times}\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\bar{\times}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\bar{\times}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\bar{\times}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\bar{\times}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\bar{\times}\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\bar{\times}E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

Using
Section 6
, the diagrams above read

\forall x,y,z\in M\colon(x\circ y)\circ z=x\circ(y\circ z),\qquad E\circ x=x=x\circ E.

(2)

A relational monoid is
partial
if
\circ
is single-valued, and it is

local

[
FJSZ23
]
if it satisfies

x\circ_{@}y\text{ and }v\in y\circ z\implies x\circ_{@}v.

(3)

A
functorial
morphism
h\colon(M,\circ,E)\rightarrow(M^{\prime},\circ,E^{\prime})
of partial monoids is a pure morphism of binary
J_{\mathcal{P}}
-operators (that is,
\mathcal{P}(h)(x\circ y)=h(x)\circ h(y)
) satisfying
h(E)\subseteq E^{\prime}
. The category of relational monoids and functorial morphisms is denoted by
\mathbf{RelMon}
.

Every monoid is obviously also a relational monoid.
An example of a partial monoid that is not local is given by
(\mathcal{P}(X),\circ,\emptyset)
,
|X|\geq 1
, with
A\circ B=A+B
if
A,B
are disjoint and
A\circ B=\emptyset
otherwise.

Remark 49
.

(1)

The full subcategory of
\mathbf{RelMon}
consisting of local partial monoids is equivalent to the category of small categories
[
ML98
,
FJSZ23
,
SS67
]
:

\mathbf{RelMon}_{\mathrm{fun},\mathrm{loc}}\simeq\mathbf{Cat}.

From right to left, a small category
\mathbf{C}
is mapped to the relational monoid
\mor{\mathbf{C}}
with identities
\{\mathrm{id}_{C}\mid C\in\mathbf{C}\}
.
It is easy to see that
\mor{\mathbf{C}}
is a local partial monoid.

For left to right, one first has to show that in a partial monoid
M
every element
f\in M
has unique left and right units
e^{l}_{f},e^{r}_{f}\in E
.
The equivalence then sends a monoid
M
to the category whose objects are given by the units
E
and with morphisms

\hom(e,e^{\prime})=\{f\in M\mid e=e^{l}_{f},\,e^{\prime}=e^{r}_{f}\}.

(2)

The multiplication of the relational monoid corresponding to a small category is single-valued, that is, it factorizes as

\mor(\mathbf{C})\times\mor(\mathbf{C})\rightarrow\mor(\mathbf{C})+\{\bot\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mor(\mathbf{C})),

its set of units can be any element of
\mathcal{P}(\mor(\mathbf{C}))
.
This is the reason why we work with the full powerset in our assumptions (
5.1
) in lieu of the maybe submonad.

From
Section 5.1
we already know relational monoids correspond to certain associative residuation CABAs,
so it remains to single out the images of local partial monoids.

{defi}

(1)

A residuation CABA is
functional
if for all atoms
a\in\at(R)
the
\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigwedge}
-morphisms
a\setminus(-)\colon R\rightarrow R
also preserve non-empty joins.
It is
local
if it satisfies
x^{?}\setminus x^{?}=\top
, where

(-)^{?}\in\mathbf{CSL}_{\bigvee}(R,R),\qquad x\mapsto\neg(x\setminus\bot).

A residuation CABA is
categorical
if it has a (not necessarily atomic!) unit
e\in R
and is associative, functional and local.

(2)

A (non-unital)
morphism
from a residuation CABA
R
to
R^{\prime}
is a complete residuation algebra morphism
h\colon R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
, that is,
h
preserves all joins and meets and satisfies

x^{\prime}\setminus f(z)=f(f^{*}(x^{\prime})\setminus z)
.
If
R,R^{\prime}
are unital residuation CABAs, then a morphism is
lax unital
if it satisfies
e^{\prime}\leq f(e)
.

We denote the category of residuation CABAs by
\mathbf{ResCABA}
and its subcategory of categorical residuation CABAs and lax unital morphisms by
\mathbf{CatResCABA}
.

Theorem 50
.

The category of small categories is dually equivalent to the category of categorical residuation CABAs:

\mathbf{Cat}\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\mathbf{CatResCABA}.

Proof 6.1
.

Restricting the results from
Section 5.1
to the order-discrete setting, we get a duality

\mathbf{ResCABA}\cong\coalg(V_{\land})=\op_{V_{\land}}^{1,2}(\mathbf{CABA})\simeq^{\mathrm{op}}\op_{J_{\mathcal{P}}}^{2,1}(\mathbf{Set}).

(6.2)

From right to left, the dual of a
J_{\mathcal{P}}
-algebra
\circ\colon M\times M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}M
is given by the residuation CABA with carrier
\widehat{M}\cong\mathcal{P}(M)
, whose left residual is given by

\setminus\colon\mathcal{P}(M)^{\partial}\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathcal{P}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(M),\qquad A\mathbin{{\boxtimes}}C\mapsto\{b\mid A\circ b\subseteq C\}.

It remains to show that this duality restricts to a duality between the category of local partial monoids and functorial homomorphisms, and the category of categorical residuation CABAs and lax unital morphisms.

(1)

We start by showing that the duality restricts on objects:
a binary
J_{\mathcal{P}}
-algebra
\circ\colon M\bar{\times}M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}M
is a local, partial monoid iff its dual residuation CABA
\widehat{M}
is unital, associative, functional and local.

(1a) Partiality of multiplication corresponds to functionality of
\widehat{M}
: By
28
restricted to non-empty joins, the residuation CABA
\widehat{M}
is functional iff its
V_{\land}
-coalgebra structure preserves non-empty joins.
An argument analogous to
12
then gives that a
V_{\land}
-coalgebra preserves non-empty joins iff its dual order-relation is a partial map.
Combining these two equivalences we get that the residuation CABA
\widehat{M}
is functional if its dual relation
\circ\colon M\times M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(M)
is a partial map.

(1b) By
29
the residuation CABA
\widehat{M}
has a unit
e\in\widehat{M}
iff its comultiplication has a counit
\epsilon\vdash e
iff its dual algebra
M
has a unit
E=e
. By the same lemma,
\widehat{M}
is associative iff its comultiplication is coassociative iff
M
is associative.

(1c) We show that the residuation CABA
\widehat{M}
satisfies
\forall A\in\widehat{M}\colon A^{?}\setminus A^{?}=\top
iff its dual algebra
M
is local:
We prepare by making some observations about the locality of the residuation CABA
\widehat{M}
.
Note that in
\widehat{M}
we have
\bot=\emptyset
and
\top=M
.
For
A\in\widehat{M}
we have

A^{?}=\neg(A\setminus\bot)=\{n\mid\neg(A\circ n\subseteq\emptyset)\}=\{n\mid A\circ_{@}n\}=\{n\mid\exists m\in A\colon m\circ_{@}n\}.

(6.3)

In particular, on atoms
m\in\widehat{M}
membership in
m^{?}
simplifies to the condition

n\in m^{?}\iff m\circ_{@}n.

(6.4)

We rewrite the locality condition using the adjunction
B\circ(-)\dashv B\setminus(-)
:

A^{?}\setminus A^{?}=M\iff M\subseteq A^{?}\setminus A^{?}\iff A^{?}\circ M\subseteq A^{?},

(6.5)

where
\circ\colon\widehat{M}\mathbin{{\otimes}}_{\mathrm{C}}\widehat{M}\rightarrow\widehat{M}
is the
V_{\lor}
-algebra structure on
\widehat{M}
corresponding to the extension
\mathcal{P}M\times\mathcal{P}M\to\mathcal{P}M
of the partial monoid multiplication.

We start with the direction that if
\widehat{M}
satisfies (
6.5
), then
M
is local:
For this, let
x,y\in M
such that
x\circ_{@}y
and
v\in y\circ z
. We have to show that
x\circ_{@}v
.
We first apply (
6.4
) to
x\circ_{@}y
to obtain
y\in x^{?}
. Monotonicity of
\circ
in both
arguments then yields the first inclusion below
and (
6.5
) the second one:

v\in y\circ z\subseteq x^{?}\circ M\subseteq x^{?}.

But by (
6.4
) this means that
x\circ_{@}v
, as
required.

For the other direction, we prove that if
M
is local, then
\widehat{M}
is local.
By (
6.5
) it suffices to prove
\forall A\in\widehat{M}\colon A^{?}\circ M\subseteq A^{?}
.
Let
v\in A^{?}\circ M
. Then there exist
y\in A^{?}
,
z\in M
such that
v\in y\circ z
.
Since
y\in A^{?}
, there exists, by (
6.3
), some
x\in A
with
x\circ_{@}y
.
Locality of
M
now implies that
x\circ_{@}v
, so
v\in A^{?}
as required using (
6.3
) again.

(2)

Finally, we show that the duality in (
6.2
) restricts on morphisms as claimed.
Let
f\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be a pure morphism of
J_{\mathcal{P}}
-algebras with dual morphism
h=f^{-1}\colon\widehat{M^{\prime}}\rightarrow\widehat{M}
of residuation CABAs.
We have to show that
f
is functorial iff
h
is lax unital:
By (1b) the unit
E
of a partial monoid
M
dualizes to the residual unit
E\in\widehat{M}
.
Now
f
is a functorial morphism of partial monoids iff
f[E]\subseteq E^{\prime}
iff
E\subseteq f^{-1}[E^{\prime}]=h(E^{\prime})
iff
h
is
lax unital.

7.
Conclusion and Future Work

We have presented an abstract approach to extending Stone-type dualities based on adjunctions
between monoidal categories and instantiated it to recover classical extended Stone and
Priestley duality, along with a generalization of it to relational morphisms. Guided by these
foundations, we have investigated residuation and derivation algebras, leading to a new duality for
Priestley monoids, and we extended this duality to include relational morphisms. In addition, we
have derived a new dual characterization of the category of small categories.

Relational morphisms are an important tool in algebraic language theory, notably for characterizing language operations algebraically. For instance, Straubing
[
Str81
]
first showed that relational morphisms are tightly connected to the concatenation product and the star operation on regular languages; see also the surveys by Pin
[
Pin88
,
Pin11
]
.
In future work, we intend to apply our duality-theoretic insights on relational morphisms to
illuminate, and possibly recover, these results from a categorical perspective, much in the
spirit of the duality-theoretic view of Eilenberg’s Variety Theorem by Gehrke
et al.
[
GGP08
]
and the categorical works it has inspired (see e.g.
[
Sal17
,
UACM17
,
Boj15
,
Blu21
]
).

Another goal is to apply our duality framework beyond
classical Stone and Priestley dualities. Specifically, we aim to
develop an extended duality theory for the recently developed

nominal
Stone duality
[
BMU23
]
, which
would enable a generalization of our present results on residuation algebras
to the nominal setting with applications to data languages.

A conceptually rather different dual characterization of the category of profinite monoids and
continuous monoid morphisms in terms of
semi-Galois categories
has
been provided by Uramoto
[
Ura16
]
. Extending this result to
relational morphisms, similar to our
47
, is another
interesting point for future work.

Potential applications of our abstract approach to extended Stone duality are not limited to
algebraic language theory. In
Section 4.3
we have used the compositionality of extended (Stone) duality to recover results from modal correspondence theory by purely categorical methods.
We hope that the monoidal approach might bring a new impulse into the historic endeavor of correspondence theory.
In future work we will investigate the expressiveness of this idea, that is, study which
relational properties can be captured by suitable inequations of operators. Applying this
approach to more complex modal axioms that, for example, involve negation or implication or combine multiple modalities, can be expected to be non-trivial.

Our applications so far were based on Stone and Priestley duality, but the general framework of abstract extended duality applies far beyond this setting. For instance, Furber and Jacobs
[
FJ15
]
showed how to extend the duality between
C^{*}
-algebras and compact Hausdorff spaces (‘Gelfand duality’) to a ‘probabilistic Gelfand duality’, which emerges by employing a weaker notion of morphism between
C^{*}
-algebras and by replacing the category of compact Hausdorff spaces by the Kleisli category of the Radon monad.
This result seems to fit perfectly into our approach of extending dualities.
A thorough instantiation of the results from
[
FJ15
]
to our framework could not only place these results in a larger categorical context but also uncover new results in probabilistic duality theory.

Fabian Lenke and Stefan Milius are supported by Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) under project 419850228. Henning Urbat is supported by Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) under project 470467389.
